## F001

**Question:** In Figure 1B-1, what happens immediately after FHWA approves a request to experiment?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1B.05, Figure 1B-1, p.7, p.8, p.6, Page 48. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=1.000. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: According to Figure 1B-1, once the FHWA review results in a "Yes" at the "Approved?" decision point, the immediate next step is that the requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device. After installation, the process branches into two parallel activities—evaluating the experimental device and providing semi-annual reports to FHWA Division & HQ—both of which lead to the requesting jurisdiction providing FHWA a copy of the final report. (If the answer is "No," the requesting jurisdiction instead responds to questions raised by FHWA, and the request loops back to FHWA Review.) Standards (mandatory): - Note that experimentation itself requires prior approval: "A traffic control device or application that does not comply with the provisions of this Manual shall not be used on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel... without first receiving official approval to experiment from the FHWA's Office of Transportation Operations." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 1B-1 shows the "Approved?" diamond with the "Yes" arrow leading directly to the box "Requesting jurisdiction installs experimental traffic control device," which then branches to "Evaluate experimental traffic control device" and "Requesting jurisdiction provides semi-annual reports to FHWA Division & HQ," both flowing into "Requesting jurisdiction provides FHWA a copy of final report." [Image 2] The full page view of Figure 1B-1 confirms the same sequence, including the "No" loop back to FHWA Review via the "Requesting jurisdiction responds to questions raised by FHWA" box. Citations: - Figure 1B-1 (p.7) - Section 1B.05 Support §9 (p.8) - Section 1B.05 Standard §2 (p.6) - Page 48

**Gold answer:** The requesting jurisdiction installs the experimental traffic control device.

## F002

**Question:** In Figure 1B-2, if a proposed change is accepted for Federal rulemaking, what process follows before a Final Rule is published?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1B.07, Section 1B.06, Section 1B.02, Figure 1B-2, p.9, p.11. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: FHWA develops provisions for a Notice of Proposed Amendments, publishes the notice in the Federal Register, holds a docket comment period, reviews comments, prepares the Final Rul… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.944 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=0.00, visual recall=0.00, and visual precision=0.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: According to Figure 1B-2, once a change is accepted for Federal rulemaking, FHWA develops device provisions for inclusion in the next Notice of Proposed Amendments, publishes that Notice in the Federal Register, holds a docket comment period, reviews the comments received, and then prepares the Final Rule before publishing it. In parallel, the flowchart shows an "Interim approval?" decision that can allow provisional use of the device (under technical conditions) while the rulemaking proceeds. After the Final Rule is published, State manuals must be brought into substantial conformance within 2 years. Standards (mandatory): - "States or other Federal agencies that have their own MUTCDs or Supplements shall revise these MUTCDs or Supplements to be in substantial conformance with changes to the National MUTCD within 2 years of the effective date of the Final Rule for the changes [23 CFR 655.603(b)(3)]." Options (permitted): - Per the interim-approval branch of Figure 1B-2 and Section 1B.07 Support: "Interim approval allows for the optional use of a traffic control device or application and does not create a new mandate or recommendation for its use," pending an official rulemaking action; jurisdictions may apply for and receive Interim Approval and deploy devices under its conditions while rulemaking proceeds. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 1B-2 shows the "YES" branch from the "Accepted for Federal rulemaking?" decision proceeding through: FHWA develops device provisions for the next Notice of Proposed Amendments → FHWA publishes Notice of Proposed Amendments in the Federal Register → Docket comment period → FHWA reviews comments → FHWA prepares Final Rule → FHWA publishes Final Rule, with a parallel Interim Approval branch (issuance with technical conditions, jurisdictions apply/receive approval and deploy devices) and a downstream box stating State manuals must be in substantial conformance within 2 years per 23 CFR 655.603(a). Citations: - Figure 1B-2 (p.9) - Section 1B.07 Support §3 (p.11) - Section 1B.07 Support §4 (p.11) - Section 1B.06 Support §4 (p.9) - Section 1B.02 Standard §2 (p.4)

**Gold answer:** FHWA develops provisions for a Notice of Proposed Amendments, publishes the notice in the Federal Register, holds a docket comment period, reviews comments, prepares the Final Rule, and publishes it.

## F004

**Question:** In Figure 2A-2, what minimum mounting height and lateral offset are shown for a basic roadside sign in a rural area without a wider shoulder?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A 5-foot minimum mounting height and a 12-foot minimum lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Figure 2A-2, Panel A ("Roadside sign in rural area") shows a minimum mounting height of 5 feet, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, and a minimum lateral offset of 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way. The 6-foot offset from the edge of the shoulder (Panel B) applies only where a shoulder wider than 6 feet exists. Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." Guidance (recommended): - "For post-mounted signs, the minimum lateral offset should be 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way. If a shoulder wider than 6 feet exists, the minimum lateral offset for post-mounted signs should be 6 feet from the edge of the shoulder." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2A-2, Panel A depicts a STOP sign in a rural area with "5 ft MIN." vertical height from the bottom of the sign to the near pavement edge elevation and "12 ft MIN." lateral offset from the traveled way, while Panel B shows the alternate "6 ft MIN." offset case labeled "Shoulder wider than 6 ft." [Image 2] The full-page view of Figure 2A-2 confirms the same 5 ft MIN. height and 12 ft MIN. offset dimensions for Panel A. [Image 5] Figure 2A-3's note reinforces that lateral offset is a minimum of 6 feet from the edge of the shoulder or 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way. [Image 3] and [Image 4] show analogous height/offset illustrations for recreational-area and temporary traffic control signs, not the basic rural case in Figure 2A-2. Citations: - Section 2A.15 Standard §4 (p.58) - Section 2A.16 Guidance §3 (p.60) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Page 92 - Page 93

**Gold answer:** A 5-foot minimum mounting height and a 12-foot minimum lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way.

The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In Figure 2A-5, the distinguishing visual feature is whether the blue paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross each other within the median. In Panel A ("Separate intersections"), the median is wide enough that the opposing left-turn paths cross one another as they traverse the interior of the crossing; in Panel B ("Single intersection"), the median is narrower and the opposing left-turn paths curve past each other without crossing. Guidance (recommended): - "A divided highway crossing should be signed and marked as separate intersections when both of the following conditions are present: A. The paths of opposing left turns from the divided highway cross each other (see Figure 2A-5), and B. There is adequate storage in the interior approaches for the design vehicles expected to cross the divided highway." - "At the crossing of two divided highways, engineering judgment should be used to determine the number of separate intersections." Standards (mandatory): - Related signing consequence: "At the crossing of a roadway with a divided highway that functions as two separate intersections, ONE WAY signs shall be placed, visible to each crossroad approach, on the near right and far left corners of each intersection with the directional roadways (see Figure 2B-19)." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2A-5 shows Panel A labeled "Separate intersections (paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross)" with a wide median where the two blue left-turn arrows intersect each other, versus Panel B labeled "Single intersection (paths of opposing left-turning vehicles do not cross)" with a narrower median where the blue left-turn arrows pass without intersecting. [Image 2] The full-page view of page 105 confirms the same two-panel contrast, with the figure captions explicitly stating whether the opposing left-turn paths cross or do not cross. Citations: - Figure 2A-5 (p.64) - Section 2A.23 Guidance §1 (p.63) - Section 2A.23 Guidance §3 (p.64) - Section 2B.49 Standard §3 (p.114) - Page 105

**Gold answer:** In the separate-intersections case, the paths of opposing left-turning vehicles cross; in the single-intersection case, those paths do not cross.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In Figure 2B-1, the STOP (R1-1) sign is an octagon with white legend and border on a red background, and the YIELD (R1-2) sign is a downward-pointing equilateral triangle with a red border band and the word YIELD in red on a white interior. The ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque is a small rectangular plaque with a white legend and border on a red background, as required by the Standard. Standards (mandatory): - "At intersections where all approaches are controlled by STOP signs (see Section 2B.12), an ALL-WAY (R1-3P) supplemental plaque (see Figure 2B-1) shall be mounted below each STOP sign. The ALL-WAY plaque shall have a white legend and border on a red background." - "When it is determined that a full stop is always required on an approach to an intersection, a STOP (R1-1) sign (see Figure 2B-1) shall be used." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2B-1 shows the octagonal red STOP sign (R1-1), the downward-pointing triangular YIELD sign (R1-2), and the red rectangular ALL-WAY plaque (R1-3P) with white legend and border. - [Image 5] Page 159 reproduces Figure 2B-1 in context, confirming the octagonal STOP sign, triangular YIELD sign, and the red ALL-WAY plaque with white legend. - [Image 2] Page 115 shows the same octagonal STOP sign shape in an intersection signing figure, consistent with Figure 2B-1. - [Image 3] Page 1015 shows the STOP octagon and YIELD triangle shapes used in school-crossing sign assemblies, consistent with their depiction in Figure 2B-1. - [Image 4] Page 835 shows the R1-1 octagonal STOP sign and R1-2 triangular YIELD sign in a temporary traffic control figure, matching the shapes in Figure 2B-1. Citations: - Section 2B.04 Standard §4 (p.74) - Section 2B.04 Standard §1 (p.74) - Figure 2B-1 (p.74) - Page 159

**Gold answer:** STOP is an octagon; YIELD is a downward-pointing equilateral triangle; the ALL-WAY plaque has white legend and border on a red background.

Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 4I-4 shows the interval sequence Walk Interval → Pedestrian Change Interval → Buffer Interval, with the Walk Interval labeled "7 seconds MIN.**" and the Buffer Interval labeled "2 seconds MIN.", plus the "Zero" point of the countdown display at the start of the Buffer Interval. Upon termination of the pedestrian change interval, the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces shall revert to a dark (not illuminated) condition." - "A pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall consist of three signal sections, with a CIRCULAR YELLOW signal indication centered below two horizontally-aligned CIRCULAR RED signal indications (see Figure 4J-3)." [Image 3] Page 770 shows the same Figure 4J-3 sequence and note in the Revision 1 page layout. **Model answer:** Direct Answer: The principal layout difference is the right-of-way relationship to the roadway. Both crossings show detectable warnings on each approach to the tracks. Standards (mandatory): - "Detectable warnings (see Chapter 3C) shall be used at pathway grade crossings where pedestrian travel is permitted and at sidewalk grade crossings and shall extend across the full width of the pathway or sidewalk." (This explains the detectable warning strips shown on both panels of the figure.) Support (informational): - A pathway "is frequently placed in its own right-of-way on an alignment that is independent of any roadway," and if built parallel to a roadway it is physically separated by open space or a barrier so that roadway grade crossing devices do not exert influence over or provide adequate warning to pathway users. - A sidewalk "runs parallel to a roadway within the highway right-of-way and is close enough to the edge of the roadway's traveled way" that roadway grade crossing devices can frequently exert an influence over or provide adequate warning to sidewalk users. - Pathways are typically used by both pedestrians and bicyclists, whereas sidewalks are typically used only by pedestrians. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 8E-1 shows panel A with a stand-alone pathway crossing the tracks inside its own labeled "Pathway right-of-way," while panel B shows the sidewalk directly adjacent to a marked roadway, both contained within a single labeled "Highway right-of-way," with detectable warnings on both approaches in each panel. [Image 2] The full page confirms the figure and the accompanying Section 8E.01 Support text explaining the pathway/sidewalk distinction. [Image 4] shows Figure 8E-2, illustrating how a sidewalk crossing relates to roadway traffic control devices, consistent with the sidewalk being within the highway right-of-way. [Image 3] and [Image 5] show pedestrian gate provisions and figures (8E-8 through 8E-10) but do not bear directly on the layout difference in Figure 8E-1. Citations: - Section 8E.01 Support §4 (p.1035) - Section 8E.04 Standard §7 (p.1038) - Figure 8E-1 (p.1035) - Page 1076 - Page 1077

**Gold answer:** The pathway crossing is on a separate pathway alignment outside the roadway, whereas the sidewalk crossing is part of the highway corridor alongside the roadway and crosses the tracks with the roadway.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD does not specify a foundation depth for the sign posts shown in Figure 2A-2. The figure and its associated provisions govern mounting heights and lateral offsets (e.g., 5-foot and 7-foot minimum heights, 12-foot and 6-foot minimum offsets), not the depth of post foundations. The only related requirement is a performance-based standard that foundations must hold signs in a proper and permanent position; no numeric depth is given in the evidence provided. Standards (mandatory): - "Sign posts, foundations, and mountings shall be so constructed as to hold signs in a proper and permanent position, and to resist swaying in the wind or displacement by vandalism." ## M001

**Question:** A provision appears under a Guidance heading and uses the word 'should.' Is it mandatory, and when may a deviation be appropriate?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1D.03, Section 6P.01, Section 1C.01, Section 2D.06, p.13, p.32. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: It is recommended practice, not a mandatory Standard. | A deviation may be appropriate when engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates that the deviation is suitable for the specific situation. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No — a provision under a Guidance heading using "should" is a recommended practice, not a mandatory requirement. Guidance describes recommended practice in typical situations, and a deviation is appropriate when engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates that the deviation is appropriate. Standards (mandatory): - "Guidance-a statement of recommended practice in typical situations, with deviations allowed if engineering judgment or engineering study (see Section 1D.03) indicates the deviation to be appropriate. All Guidance statements are labeled, and the text appears in unbold italic type. The verb 'should' is typically used. The verbs 'shall' and 'may' are not used in Guidance statements. Guidance statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." - By contrast, "Standard-a statement of required, mandatory, or specifically prohibitive practice regarding a traffic control device… The verb 'shall' is typically used. The verbs 'should' and 'may' are not used in Standard statements." Guidance (recommended): - Typical Guidance provisions use "should" to express recommended practice — for example, "guide signs should be limited to no more than three lines of destinations" — illustrating the recommended (not mandatory) nature of such statements. Support (explanatory): - Section 1D.03 explains that the application of engineering study and engineering judgment is a fundamental principle of the use of traffic control devices, which underpins how deviations from recommended practice are evaluated and documented. - Section 6P.01 notes that, except for clearly labeled notes, the information presented in typical applications can generally be regarded as Guidance — i.e., recommended, not mandatory. Citations: - Section 1C.01 Standard §1 (p.13) - Section 1D.03 Support §2 (p.32) - Section 2D.06 Guidance §2 (p.209) - Section 6P.01 Support §2 (p.857)

**Gold answer:** It is recommended practice, not a mandatory Standard. A deviation may be appropriate when engineering judgment or an engineering study indicates that the deviation is suitable for the specific situation.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The strip must be at least 2 inches wide, extend from the sign down the full support to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, match the sign background color except th… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Using a retroreflective strip on a sign support is a permitted (optional) method of enhancing sign conspicuity, chosen based on engineering judgment. When used, the strip must be at least 2 inches wide, run the full length of the support from the sign down to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, carry no legend, and match the sign's background color — except that the strip for YIELD and DO NOT ENTER signs must be red. A related mandatory application (white strip) exists for Crossbuck supports at passive grade crossings. Standards (mandatory): - "If a strip of retroreflective material is used on the sign support, it shall be at least 2 inches in width, it shall be placed for the full length of the support from the sign to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, and its color shall match the background color of the sign, except that the color of the strip for the YIELD and DO NOT ENTER signs shall be red. The retroreflective strip shall not display any legend or other information." - For passive grade crossings: "A vertical strip of retroreflective white material, not less than 2 inches in width, shall be used on each Crossbuck support at passive grade crossings for the full length of the back of the support from the Crossbuck sign or Number of Tracks plaque to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, except as provided in Paragraph 18 of this Section." A white strip wrapped around a round support of at least 2 inches outside diameter satisfies this requirement. Options (permitted): - Based upon engineering judgment, where improvement of the conspicuity of a standard regulatory, warning, or guide sign is desired, methods shown in Figure 2A-1 — including a vertical retroreflective strip on the sign support — may be used, as appropriate. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-1, panel E, illustrates a "Vertical retroreflective strip on sign support" beneath a fluorescent yellow-green pedestrian warning sign, with the strip color matching the sign background and a dimension callout of "2 ft MAX." between the bottom of the strip and the near edge of the roadway. - [Image 2] The full-page view of Figure 2A-1 (labeled Sect. 2A.11) shows the same panel E strip detail in the context of the seven conspicuity-enhancement examples (A–G). - [Image 3] Page 103 shows Section 2A.21 Support text noting that "Information regarding the use of retroreflective material on the sign support is contained in Section 2A.11." - [Image 4] Page 90 shows the Section 2A.11 Standard text (Paragraph 5) with the 2-inch width, full-length placement, color-matching, and no-legend requirements, plus item L listing the strip as a conspicuity method. - [Image 5] Page 1038 shows the Crossbuck Assembly figure with callouts for an "Optional 2-inch red or white retroreflective strip on front of support" and a required "2-inch white retroreflective strip on back of support," with the "2 ft MAX." dimension measured to the near edge of the roadway. Citations: - Section 2A.11 Standard §5 (p.49) - Section 2A.11 Option §1 (p.49) - Section 2A.21 Support §8 (p.62) - Section 8B.04 Standard §17 (p.1000) - Figure 2A-1 (p.48) - Page 89 - Page 90 - Page 103 - Page 1038

**Gold answer:** The strip must be at least 2 inches wide, extend from the sign down the full support to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway, match the sign background color except that YIELD and DO NOT ENTER strips are red, and contain no legend or other information.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For a basic roadside sign in a rural area, the normal minimum mounting height is 5 feet, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement. The normal minimum lateral offset shown in Figure 2A-2 is 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way (or 6 feet from the edge of a shoulder that is wider than 6 feet). Where terrain or vegetation make this impractical, Section 2A.16 permits a reduced lateral offset of at least 2 feet. Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - By contrast, "In business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely to occur, or where the view of the sign might be obstructed, the minimum height... of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 7 feet (see Figure 2A-2)" — confirming that the 5-foot value applies specifically to the rural case. Guidance (recommended): - The figure note accompanying Section 2A.13 explains the dimensioning convention: "Lateral offset is a minimum of 6 feet measured from the edge of the shoulder, or 12 feet measured from the edge of the traveled way. See Section 2A.16 for lower minimums that may be used in urban areas, or where lateral offset space is limited." Options (permitted): - "On conventional, low-volume rural, and special-purpose roads in areas where it is impractical to locate a sign with the lateral offset prescribed by this Section because of roadside features such as terrain or vegetation, a lateral offset of at least 2 feet may be used." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2A-2 diagrams A and B ("Roadside sign in rural area") show a "5 ft MIN." mounting height together with a "12 ft MIN." lateral offset from the edge of the traveled way, or a "6 ft MIN." offset where the "Shoulder [is] wider than 6 ft." [Image 2] Page 92 reproduces the same Figure 2A-2 rural diagrams (A and B) with the 5 ft minimum height and 12 ft/6 ft lateral offsets. [Image 4] Page 100 contains Section 2A.15 mounting-height provisions and the beginning of Section 2A.16 Lateral Offset, noting that "Figure 2A-2 illustrates some examples of the mounting height requirements contained in this Section." [Image 5] Page 93 (Figure 2A-3) carries the note that lateral offset is a minimum of 6 feet from the edge of the shoulder, or 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way. [Image 3] Page 566 (Figure 2M-3) similarly shows rural roadside assemblies with 5 ft minimum height and 12 ft minimum lateral offset, consistent with the rural convention. Citations: - Section 2A.15 Standard §4 (p.58) - Section 2A.15 Standard §5 (p.58) - Section 2A.13 Guidance §6 (p.57) - Section 2A.16 Option §9 (p.60) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Page 92 - Page 93 - Page 100 - Page 566

**Gold answer:** The minimum mounting height is 5 feet. The normal minimum lateral offset is 12 feet from the edge of the traveled way, or 6 feet from the edge of a shoulder wider than 6 feet.

Section 1D.08 mandates that Table 1D-3 abbreviations shall not be used on any traffic control device, and this requirement applies to changeable message signs through Section 2L.05. Standards (mandatory): - "The abbreviations shown in Table 1D-3 shall not be used in connection with traffic control devices because of their potential to be misinterpreted by road users." - "Abbreviations displayed on CMS shall comply with the provisions of Section 1D.08." - If "Delay" needs to be abbreviated on a portable changeable message sign, only an abbreviation authorized by Table 1D-2 may be used: "When the word messages shown in Table 1D-2 need to be abbreviated on a portable changeable message sign, the abbreviations shown in Table 1D-2 shall be used." Guidance (recommended): - If any Table 1D-2 abbreviation with a prompt word were used instead, "the abbreviations for the words listed in Table 1D-2 that also show a prompt word should not be used on a portable changeable message sign... unless the prompt word shown in Table 1D-2 either precedes or follows the abbreviation, as applicable." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 1D-3, "Unacceptable Abbreviations," which lists DLY with intended word "Delay" and common misinterpretation "Daily," confirming the proposed abbreviation is expressly prohibited. Citations: - Section 1D.08 Standard §4 (p.34) - Section 2L.05 Standard §6 (p.516) - Section 1D.08 Standard §2 (p.34) - Section 1D.08 Guidance §3 (p.34) - Table 1D-3 (p.37)

**Gold answer:** No. DLY is explicitly prohibited because it can be misinterpreted as DAILY.

## M006

**Question:** Compare the quantitative triggers in the four-leg All-Way Stop crash warrant and the 8-hour volume warrant.

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=0.75, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=No

The official rubric did not award strict full credit because at least one dimension scored below 1.00. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit found incomplete coverage even though the automated clause comparison did not isolate a single missing sentence. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2B.13, Section 2B.16, p.79. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The crash warrant may apply with at least 5 correctable crashes in 12 months or 6 in 36 months. | The volume warrant may apply when, for the same 8 hours, major-street entering volume is at least 300 units/hour and minor-street entering volume at least 200 units/hour, with a p… Adjudication note: Correct crash and eight-hour volume thresholds; omits the 70-percent high-speed adjustment. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 2, which produced MRR=0.500. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.624 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Both warrants are Option (permitted) provisions that allow all-way stop control based on an engineering study, but they measure different phenomena. The four-leg crash warrant is triggered by crash frequency — five or more reported correctable crashes in 12 months, or six or more in 36 months. The 8-hour volume warrant (Warrant D) is triggered by traffic volume — at least 300 combined vehicle/bicycle/pedestrian units per hour entering from the major-street approaches, and at least 200 units per hour from the minor-street approaches, during each of the same 8 hours of a typical day. Options (permitted): - Crash warrant (four-leg): "All-way stop control may be installed at an intersection where an engineering study indicates that: A. For a four-leg intersection, there are five or more reported crashes in a 12-month period or six or more reported crashes in a 36-month period that were of a type susceptible to correction by the installation of all-way stop control." - 8-hour volume warrant: All-way stop control may be installed where the study indicates "A. The combined motor vehicle, bicycle, and pedestrian volume entering the intersection from the major-street approaches is at least 300 units per hour for each of any 8 hours of a typical day; and B. The combined motor vehicle, bicycle, and pedestrian volume entering the intersection from the minor-street approaches is at least 200 units per hour for each of any of the same 8 hours." - Key quantitative comparison: the crash warrant uses discrete event counts over long observation windows (5 crashes/12 months or 6 crashes/36 months), while the volume warrant uses hourly flow thresholds (300 and 200 units per hour) that must be met concurrently — both conditions during the same 8 hours. The crash warrant conditions are alternatives (either time window suffices), whereas the volume warrant's two conditions are joint ("and"). Citations: - Section 2B.13 Option §1 (p.79) - Section 2B.16 Option §1 (p.79)

**Gold answer:** The crash warrant may apply with at least 5 correctable crashes in 12 months or 6 in 36 months. The volume warrant may apply when, for the same 8 hours, major-street entering volume is at least 300 units/hour and minor-street entering volume at least 200 units/hour, with a possible 70-percent adjustment if major-street 85th-percentile speed exceeds 40 mph.

Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2A.22, Table 2A-5, p.62, p.63. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: For white on red, the table gives white at least 35, red at least 7 cd/lx/m², and at least a 3:1 white-to-red contrast ratio. | The agency complies by having and using a method designed to maintain the minimums, even if some individual signs fall below them at a particular moment. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.975 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The Standard requires the agency to have and use an assessment or management method designed to maintain retroreflectivity at or above Table 2A-5 minimums; the Support text explicitly states that an agency using such a method remains in compliance even if some individual signs are below the minimums at a particular point in time. Signs identified by the agency's method as substandard should be replaced. For white-on-red signs such as STOP signs, Table 2A-5 requires a minimum maintained white retroreflectivity of ≥ 35 and red of ≥ 7 cd/lx/m², with a minimum sign contrast ratio of at least 3:1 (white ÷ red). Standards (mandatory): - "Public agencies or officials having jurisdiction shall use an assessment or management method that is designed to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the minimum levels in Table 2A-5." Guidance (recommended): - Except for signs identified in Paragraph 5, one or more of the methods described in "Maintaining Traffic Sign Retroreflectivity" (FHWA-SA-07-020, Revised 2013), or a method developed based on an engineering study, should be used to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the Table 2A-5 minimums; "Signs that are identified through the agency’s method as being below the minimum levels should be replaced." Options (permitted): - Highway agencies may exclude certain signs from the retroreflectivity maintenance guidelines: Parking, Standing, and Stopping (R7 and R8 series) signs; Walking/Hitchhiking/Crossing (R9 series, R10-1 through R10-4b) signs; Acknowledgment signs; and Bikeway signs intended for exclusive use by bicyclists or pedestrians. (Note: this exclusion list does not include STOP-type signs.) Support (informational): - "Compliance with the Standard in Paragraph 2 of this Section is achieved by having a method in place and using the method to maintain the minimum levels established in Table 2A-5. Provided that an assessment or management method is being used, an agency or official having jurisdiction would be in compliance with the Standard in Paragraph 2 of this Section even if there are some individual signs that do not meet the minimum retroreflectivity levels at a particular point in time." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2A-5 with the White on Red row, giving minimum maintained levels of W ≥ 35; R ≥ 7 cd/lx/m² (measured at 0.2° observation angle and −4.0° entrance angle), subject to the footnote requiring a minimum sign contrast ratio ≥ 3:1 (white retroreflectivity ÷ red retroreflectivity). Citations: - Section 2A.22 Standard §2 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Support §3 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Guidance §4 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Option §5 (p.63) - Table 2A-5 (p.62)

**Gold answer:** Not automatically. The agency complies by having and using a method designed to maintain the minimums, even if some individual signs fall below them at a particular moment. For white on red, the table gives white at least 35, red at least 7 cd/lx/m², and at least a 3:1 white-to-red contrast ratio.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. On a 35-mph roadway the reduced ("Minimum") sizes may not be used, because the MUTCD restricts sizes smaller than the Conventional Road sizes to low-speed facilities with operating speeds of 30 mph or less; at 35 mph the full Conventional Road (or larger) sizes govern. However, the evidence provided does not include the regulatory sign size table for the STOP sign (R1-1) itself, so I cannot state the exact required STOP sign dimensions for this situation from this evidence. Guidance (recommended): - For school-area regulatory signs, "The sizes in the Oversized column should be used on roadways that have four or more lanes with posted speed limits of 40 mph or higher" (not triggered at 35 mph, where Conventional Road sizes apply). Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 2D-2 (Sheet 1, Post-Mounted Signs) distinguishes Single-Lane and Multi-Lane letter/numeral sizes for guide signs by speed range (e.g., 30–40 mph columns), showing that multi-lane roads generally require larger legend sizes than single-lane roads at the same speed. - [Image 2] Table 7B-1 lists Conventional Road, Minimum, and Oversized sizes for school-area signs and plaques, and its note states that minimum sign sizes for multi-lane conventional roads shall be as shown in the Conventional Road column — reinforcing that reduced sizes are not for multi-lane roads. - [Image 5] shows the Section 2A.07 Standard text restricting Minimum-column sizes to low-speed roadways with operating speeds of 30 mph or less. - [Image 3], [Image 4], [Image 6], [Image 7], and [Image 8] do not provide the regulatory-sign size table (e.g., R1-1 dimensions) needed to state the exact STOP sign size. Citations: - Section 2A.07 Standard §2 (p.45) - Section 7B.01 Guidance §3 (p.972) - Section 2D.07 Guidance §5 (p.210) - Table 2D-2 (p.210) - Table 7B-1 (p.971)

**Gold answer:** For regulatory signs other than STOP, the single-lane size may be used at 35 mph or less. The STOP-sign exception remains: the multi-lane STOP size is 36 x 36 inches.

The minimum distance between the signs shall be in accordance with Section 2C.04 and Table 2C-3." Guidance (recommended): - Table 2C-3 lists, for 55 mph under the potential stop condition (Condition B, 0-mph column): 495 feet ("55 mph 990 ft 495 ft 275 ft 225 ft 200 ft 125 ft N/A5 - - -"). - Per Table 2C-3 footnote 3, the potential stop condition distances "are based on the 2018 AASHTO Policy, Table 3-1, Stopping Sight Distance, providing a PRT of 2.5 seconds, a deceleration rate of 11.2 feet/second²," and per footnote 1, "warning signs with less than 6-inch legend or more than four words, a minimum of 100 feet should be added to the advance placement distance." - "The effectiveness of the placement of warning signs should be periodically evaluated under both day and night conditions." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2C-3 with the 55-mph row listing 495 ft in the "0³" column (the potential stop condition per footnote 3). [Image 2] shows Section 2C.04, where the Guidance statement notes that the distances in Table 2C-3 should be applied with engineering judgment, and includes Table 2C-3 titled "Guidelines for Advance Placement of Warning Signs." [Image 5] shows Figure 2C-2 (horizontal alignment signs) with the note "See Table 2C-3 for advance placement distance guidelines," reinforcing the table's status as guidelines. Citations: - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §804 (p.153) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §5 (p.152) - Section 2C.35 Standard §1 (p.177) - Section 8B.06 Standard §3 (p.1001) - Page 193 - Page 230 - Page 226 - Page 197

**Gold answer:** The table shows 495 feet. It is a guideline to be applied with engineering judgment and may be adjusted for roadway features, other signing, and visibility; it is not an inflexible universal mandate.

If used, the signs must be installed on the outside of the curve, facing approaching traffic at roughly a right angle, with a minimum mounting height of 4 feet. Guidance (recommended): - "The approximate spacing of Chevron Alignment signs on the turn or curve measured from the point of curvature (PC) should be as shown in Table 2C-5." For advisory speeds of 35 to 45 mph and curve radii of 401 to 700 feet, Table 2C-5 indicates a sign spacing of 120 feet. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 2C-5 shows the row "35 to 45 mph / 401 to 700 feet / 120 feet," which covers a 40-mph advisory speed, and notes that the radius–advisory speed relationship in the table should not be used to determine the advisory speed. - [Image 2] Figure 2C-2 (Sheet 1 of 2, A – Curve) illustrates W1-8L and W1-8R Chevron Alignment signs placed on the outside of the curve facing each direction of approaching traffic, with a note to see Table 2C-5 for spacing of W1-8 signs. - [Image 3] This page shows the Section 2C.08 text with Table 2C-5, including the 35 to 45 mph / 401 to 700 feet / 120 feet row and adjacent Standard provisions on chevron placement restrictions. - [Image 4] This page presents the full Section 2C.08 Standard, Guidance, and Option text alongside Table 2C-5, confirming the outside-of-curve placement, right-angle orientation, 4-foot minimum height, and 120-foot spacing for the given speed/radius. - [Image 5] This page (Section 2C.05/2C.06) lists Chevron Alignment (W1-8) signs among treatments for changes in horizontal alignment and references device selection criteria in Table 2C-4. - [Image 6] Figure 3G-1 shows delineator placement on a curve and notes that Chevron Alignment signs (W1-8) may be used instead of or in addition to standard delineators. **Gold answer:** Typical spacing is 120 feet. The signs must be on the outside of the curve, approximately at right angles to approaching traffic, with a minimum mounting height of 4 feet.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: At a 55-mph curve, no-passing zone markings must be used wherever the passing sight distance is less than the minimum value shown in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit (the specific 55-mph threshold from Table 3B-1 is not included in the evidence provided). The zone must be established based on the curve and marked with the one-direction or two-direction no-passing markings. If the next no-passing zone begins less than 400 feet away, the recommended practice is to connect the two zones with continuous no-passing markings. Standards (mandatory): - "On roadways with center line markings, no-passing zone markings shall be used at horizontal or vertical curves where the passing sight distance is less than the minimum shown in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit." - "On two-way, two-lane or three-lane roadways where center line markings are installed, no-passing zones shall be established at vertical and horizontal curves and other locations where an engineering study indicates that passing must be prohibited because of inadequate sight distances or other special conditions." - "No-passing zones shall be marked by either the one-direction no-passing zone pavement markings or the two-direction no-passing zone pavement markings described in Section 3B.01 and shown in Figures 3B-1 and 3B-3." Guidance (recommended): - "Where the distance between successive no-passing zones is less than 400 feet, no-passing zone markings should connect the zones." Thus, if the next no-passing zone begins less than 400 feet after the curve's zone ends, the two zones should be joined into one continuous no-passing zone. Citations: - Section 3B.03 Standard §4 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §3 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §1 (p.542) - Section 3B.03 Guidance §7 (p.543) - Section 2B.36 Support §3 (p.102) Note: The evidence does not include the actual passing sight distance value from Table 3B-1 for 55 mph, so the specific distance threshold cannot be quoted here. (The tabular data from Section 2C.04 concerns advance placement of warning signs, not passing sight distance, and is not applicable to this question.)

**Gold answer:** The no-passing zone must be marked when available passing sight distance is less than 900 feet. If successive no-passing zones are separated by less than 400 feet, the markings should connect them.

(The numeric values of Table 4C-2 are not reproduced in the evidence provided.) In addition to the crash thresholds, two further categories of conditions must be met: (A) an adequate trial of alternatives with satisfactory observance and enforcement has failed to reduce crash frequency, and (C) a vehicular volume condition applying for each of any 8 hours of an average day. Adequate trial of alternatives with satisfactory observance and enforcement has failed to reduce the crash frequency; and B. At least one of the following conditions applies to the reported crash history ... 1. The number of reported angle crashes and pedestrian crashes within a 1-year period equals or exceeds the threshold number in Table 4C-2 for total angle crashes and pedestrian crashes (all severities); or 2. The number of reported fatal-and-injury angle crashes and pedestrian crashes within a 1-year period equals or exceeds the threshold number in Table 4C-2 for total fatal-and-injury angle crashes and pedestrian crashes; ... and C. For each of any 8 hours of an average day, the ve[hicular volume condition — text truncated in the evidence]." - Note: Section 2B.11 (three or more reported crashes in a 12-month period for a four-leg intersection) applies to consideration of minor-road STOP control, not to the Crash Experience signal warrant, and should not be confused with the Warrant 7 thresholds. Options (permitted): - If the posted or statutory speed limit or the 85th-percentile speed on the major street exceeds 40 mph, or if the intersection lies within the built-up area of an isolated community with a population of less than 10,000, Table 4C-4 may be used in place of Table 4C-2 for the one-year crash thresholds, and the 56 percent volume columns in Table 4C-1 may be used in place of the 80 percent columns. - Agencies may calibrate HSM safety performance functions to their own crash data or develop their own SPFs, and, when documented in the engineering study, those agency-specific crash frequency values may be used instead of the values in Tables 4C-2 through 4C-5. **Gold answer:** The thresholds are at least 5 all-severity angle/pedestrian crashes or at least 3 fatal-and-injury angle/pedestrian crashes. In addition, an adequate trial of alternatives must have failed, and the applicable 80-percent vehicular-volume or pedestrian-volume condition must be met for the required hours.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Per Figure 4I-4, the pedestrian intervals occur in this order: a Walk Interval (steady WALKING PERSON, 7 seconds minimum), followed immediately by a Pedestrian Change Interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND, with optional countdown for intervals of 7 seconds or less), followed by a Buffer Interval (steady UPRAISED HAND) of at least 2 seconds before conflicting traffic is released. The buffer interval shall always be provided and displayed. Pedestrian clearance time is normally calculated using a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second, with a slower speed considered where slower pedestrians or wheelchair users routinely cross. Standards (mandatory): - Per the figure note in Figure 4I-4: "The Buffer Interval, which shall always be provided and displayed, may be used to help satisfy the calculated pedestrian clearance time, or may begin after the calculated pedestrian clearance time has ended." - The full-page evidence [Image 3] additionally shows the Standard that "a buffer interval consisting of a steady UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) signal indication shall be displayed for at least 2 seconds prior to the release of any conflicting vehicular movement," and that the sum of the pedestrian change interval and buffer interval shall not be less than the calculated pedestrian clearance time. Guidance (recommended): - "The pedestrian clearance time should be sufficient to allow a pedestrian crossing in the crosswalk who left the curb or edge of pavement at the end of the WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication to travel at a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second to at least the far side of the traveled way or to a median of sufficient width for pedestrians to wait." - Where pedestrians who walk slower than 3.5 feet per second, or pedestrians who use wheelchairs, routinely use the crosswalk, a walking speed of less than 3.5 feet per second should be considered in determining the pedestrian clearance time. Options (permitted): - The additional time provided by an extended push button press to satisfy pedestrian clearance time needs may be added to either the walk interval or the pedestrian change interval. - The countdown display is optional for Pedestrian Change Intervals of 7 seconds or less (Figure 4I-4 note). - The Buffer Interval may be used to help satisfy the calculated pedestrian clearance time, or may begin after the calculated pedestrian clearance time has ended (Figure 4I-4 note). Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 4I-4 shows the interval sequence — steady WALK (7 seconds MIN.), flashing UPRAISED HAND with countdown (Pedestrian Change Interval spanning the calculated pedestrian clearance time), then a steady-hand Buffer Interval of 2 seconds MIN. — and five possible relationships to the vehicular green/yellow/red intervals. - [Image 2] Page 765 reproduces Figure 4I-4 in context with Guidance ¶07 on the 3.5 feet per second walking speed and Option ¶08 permitting up to 4 feet per second with an extended push button press. - [Image 3] Page 764 shows the Standard requiring the pedestrian change interval to begin immediately after the WALK indication and the buffer interval (steady UPRAISED HAND) to be displayed for at least 2 seconds prior to release of any conflicting vehicular movement. - [Image 4] Page 767 contains related Guidance on minimum WALK time where a leading pedestrian interval is used, but is not central to this question. - [Image 5] Page 771 addresses pedestrian hybrid beacon operation and is not central to this question. Citations: - Section 4I.06 Support §6 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §7 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Option §9 (p.725) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §10 (p.725) - Figure 4I-4 (p.724) - Page 765 - Page 764

**Gold answer:** The order is WALK, flashing DONT WALK pedestrian change interval, then steady DONT WALK buffer interval. The buffer interval is at least 2 seconds before conflicting traffic is released, and clearance is normally evaluated using 3.5 feet per second.

Upon … termination of the pedestrian change interval, the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces shall revert to a dark (not illuminated) condition." - "A pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall consist of three signal sections, with a CIRCULAR YELLOW signal indication centered below two horizontally-aligned CIRCULAR RED signal indications (see Figure 4J-3)." - "Except as otherwise provided in this Section, a pedestrian hybrid beacon shall meet the provisions of Chapters 4D through 4G, 4I, and 4J." Options (permitted): - Per the note in Figure 4J-3: "An optional steady red clearance interval may be added after Interval 3, and an optional short buffer interval (alternating flashing red while the pedestrian signal heads are displaying a steady UPRAISED HAND) may be added after Interval 5." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 4J-3 depicts the six numbered intervals of the beacon sequence, explicitly labeling Interval 4 (two illuminated steady red indications, "SR") as "Steady Red During Pedestrian Walk Interval" and Interval 5 (alternating flashing red, "FR") as the pedestrian change interval. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 5A.03, Section 1C.03, Table 5A-1, Page 801, Page 800, p.760. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Level 3 performs all aspects within its ODD, subject to a human fallback request. | Level 2 performs steering and acceleration/deceleration but still expects the human to perform all remaining aspects of the task, so it is ADAS rather than ADS. | An ADS performs the entire dynamic driving task on a sustained basis and the term applies to Levels 3-5. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: By definition, an Automated Driving System (ADS) must be capable of performing the *entire* Dynamic Driving Task (DDT) on a sustained basis, and the term is used specifically for Level 3, 4, or 5 systems. At Level 2, the system executes both steering and acceleration/deceleration, but "the human driver performs all remaining aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task" — so the system does not perform the entire DDT, and Table 5A-1 accordingly classifies Levels 1–2 under the Advanced Driver Assistance Systems (ADAS) category. At Level 3, the system performs "all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD" (with the driver expected to respond to a request to intervene), which satisfies the entire-DDT criterion, so Table 5A-1 places Levels 3–5 under the Automated Driving System (ADS) category. Standards (mandatory): - No Standard provisions govern this classification; the acronym meaning is fixed by Standard: "ADS-Automated Driving System" (the substantive definition and table are Support material). Support (informational, from Section 5A.03 and Table 5A-1): - "Automated Driving System (ADS) – The hardware and software that are collectively capable of performing the entire Dynamic Driving Task (DDT) on a sustained basis, regardless of whether it is limited to a specific Operational Design Domain (ODD); this term is used specifically to describe a Level 3, 4, or 5 driving automation system." - Table 5A-1, Level 2: "The driving mode specific execution by one or more sustained driver assistance systems of both steering and acceleration/deceleration … with the expectation that the human driver performs all remaining aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task" — Automation Category: Advanced Driver Assistance Systems (ADAS). - Table 5A-1, Level 3: "The driving mode specific sustained performance by an ADS of all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene" — Automation Category: Automated Driving System (ADS). Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 5A-1 shows Levels 1–2 grouped under the "Advanced Driver Assistance Systems (ADAS)" category and Levels 3–5 grouped under the "Automated Driving System (ADS)" category, with the Level 2 description assigning remaining DDT aspects to the human driver and the Level 3 description assigning all DDT aspects to an ADS. - [Image 2] Page 801 shows Table 5A-1 in context, confirming the ADAS/ADS category split between Level 2 and Level 3. - [Image 4] Page 800 shows the Section 5A.03 definition of ADS (Item B) — capable of performing the entire DDT and used specifically for Level 3, 4, or 5 systems — immediately above Table 5A-1. Citations: - Section 5A.03 Support §1 (p.760) - Table 5A-1 (p.760) - Section 1C.03 Standard §1 (p.31) - Page 800 - Page 801

**Gold answer:** An ADS performs the entire dynamic driving task on a sustained basis and the term applies to Levels 3-5. Level 2 performs steering and acceleration/deceleration but still expects the human to perform all remaining aspects of the task, so it is ADAS rather than ADS. Level 3 performs all aspects within its ODD, subject to a human fallback request.

All of these descriptions are Support (informational) provisions rather than mandatory requirements. Support (informational): - "A TTC zone is often divided into four areas as needed, based on engineering judgment: the advance warning area, the transition area, the activity area, and the termination area. Figure 6B-1 illustrates the four areas typically included in a TTC zone." - "The activity area is the section of the highway where the work activity takes place. It is comprised of the work space, the traffic space, and the buffer space." - "The traffic space is the portion of the highway in which road users are routed through the activity area." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 6B-1 shows the four component areas in travel order from bottom (upstream) to top (downstream) — the Advance Warning Area ("tells traffic what to expect ahead"), the Transition Area ("moves traffic out of its normal path"), the Activity Area ("is where work takes place") containing the work space (hatched), the traffic space ("allows traffic to pass through the activity area"), and lateral/longitudinal buffer spaces ("provides protection for traffic and workers"), and finally the Termination Area ("lets traffic resume normal operations"). Citations: - Section 6B.03 Support §1 (p.771) - Section 6B.06 Support §1 (p.773) - Section 6B.06 Support §5 (p.773) - Figure 6B-1 (p.772)

**Gold answer:** Advance warning, transition, activity, and termination. The activity area consists of the work space, traffic space, and buffer space.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: For a speed of 35 mph (40 mph or less), Table 6B-4 gives L = WS²/60 = (12 × 35²)/60 = (12 × 1,225)/60 = 245 feet. Guidance (recommended): - "The appropriate taper length (L) should be determined using the criteria shown in Tables 6B-3 and 6B-4." - "A shifting taper should have a length of approximately ½ L (see Tables 6B-3 and 6B-4)." - "If used, shoulder tapers should have a length of approximately ¹⁄3 L (see Tables 6B-3 and 6B-4). If a shoulder is used as a travel lane, either through practice or during a TTC activity, a normal merging or shifting taper should be used." - Related marking guidance confirms the formula split: "For roadways where the speed limit is less than 45 mph, the formula L = WS²/60 should be used to compute the taper length," where W is the width of the offset in feet and S is the speed in mph. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 6B-4 shows the formulas: for "40 mph or less," L = WS²/60; for "45 mph or more," L = WS, where L = taper length in feet, W = width of offset in feet, and S = posted speed limit, off-peak 85th-percentile speed, or anticipated operating speed in mph. - [Image 2] Table 6B-3 lists taper length criteria: Merging Taper "at least L," Shifting Taper "at least 0.5 L," Shoulder Taper "at least 0.33 L," with a note to use Table 6B-4 to calculate L. - [Image 3] Figure 6B-2 illustrates the merging taper (L), shifting tapers (½ L), and shoulder taper (⅓ L) in plan view, noting "See Table 6B-4 for formulas for calculating taper length (L)." - [Image 4] Figure 6P-39 (TA-39) shows shoulder tapers and taper dimensions of L and ½ L applied in a median crossover layout, referencing Table 6B-4 for calculating L. - [Image 5] Figure 6P-38 (TA-38) shows an interior lane closure using tapers dimensioned as L and ½ L with a shoulder taper, referencing Table 6B-4 for L. - [Image 6] Figure 6P-42 (TA-42) shows merging tapers of length L and shoulder tapers near an exit ramp, referencing Table 6B-4 for calculating taper length (L). Citations: - Table 6B-4 (p.775) - Table 6B-3 (p.775) - Section 6B.08 Guidance §4 (p.775) - Section 6B.08 Guidance §8 (p.776) - Section 6B.08 Guidance §10 (p.776) - Section 6B.08 Support §2 (p.775) - Section 3B.13 Guidance §2 (p.568) - Page 815 - Page 978 - Page 976 - Page 984

**Gold answer:** For 40 mph or less, L = W*S²/60 = 12*35²/60 = 245 feet. Minimum merging taper: 245 feet; shifting taper: 122.5 feet; shoulder taper: about 80.9 feet (0.33L, commonly rounded appropriately in design).

## M020

**Question:** Using Section 6D.02 and Figure 6D-1, which hand-signaling device is preferred, and how should it be displayed to stop traffic?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 6D.02, Section 6D.05, Table 6G-1, Figure 6D-1, p.783, p.785. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The STOP/SLOW paddle is the primary and preferred device. | To stop traffic, the flagger faces traffic, displays the STOP face in a stationary position, and raises the free arm with the palm toward traffic. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.876 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The STOP/SLOW paddle is the primary and preferred hand-signaling device because it gives road users more positive guidance than red flags. To stop traffic, the flagger must face road users and aim the STOP paddle face toward them in a stationary position with the arm extended horizontally, while holding the free arm with the palm above shoulder level toward approaching traffic. Standards (mandatory): - "To stop road users, the flagger shall face road users and aim the STOP paddle face toward road users in a stationary position with the arm extended horizontally away from the body. The free arm shall be held with the palm of the hand above shoulder level toward approaching traffic." Guidance (recommended): - "The STOP/SLOW paddle (see Figure 6D-1 and Table 6G-1) should be the primary and preferred hand-signaling device because the STOP/SLOW paddle gives road users more positive guidance than red flags." Support (informational): - The optimum method of displaying a STOP or SLOW message is to place the STOP/SLOW paddle on a rigid staff tall enough that, when the end of the staff rests on the ground, the message is high enough to be seen by approaching or stopped traffic. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 6D-1 labels the STOP/SLOW paddle column "PREFERRED METHOD" (with the flag reserved for "EMERGENCY SITUATIONS ONLY") and depicts the "TO STOP TRAFFIC" posture — the flagger holds the R1-1 STOP paddle (18 inches minimum) on a staff with one arm while the free hand is raised, palm toward traffic. Citations: - Section 6D.02 Guidance §1 (p.783) - Section 6D.05 Standard §3 (p.785) - Section 6D.02 Support §5 (p.783) - Figure 6D-1 (p.784)

**Gold answer:** The STOP/SLOW paddle is the primary and preferred device. To stop traffic, the flagger faces traffic, displays the STOP face in a stationary position, and raises the free arm with the palm toward traffic.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Per Figure 6L-3, a Type C arrow board must have a minimum size of 96 x 48 inches, a minimum legibility distance of 1 mile, and a minimum of 15 elements. Like all arrow boards, it must provide three categories of display modes: (A) a Flashing Arrow, Sequential Arrow, or Sequential Chevron mode; (B) a flashing Double Arrow mode; and (C) a flashing Caution or Alternating Diamond mode. Standards (mandatory): - "Arrow boards shall meet the minimum size, legibility distance, number of elements, and other specifications shown in Figure 6L-3." - "An arrow board shall have the following three mode selections: A. A Flashing Arrow, Sequential Arrow, or Sequential Chevron mode; B. A flashing Double Arrow mode; and C. A flashing Caution or Alternating Diamond mode." - "The arrow board shall have suitable elements capable of the various operating modes. The color presented by the elements shall be yellow." Support (informational): - Type C arrow boards are intended to be used on high-speed, high-volume motor vehicle traffic control projects. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 6L-3 shows the specification table listing Type C at a minimum size of 96 x 48 inches, 1-mile minimum legibility distance, and 15 minimum elements, and illustrates (using a Type C board) the Flashing Arrow, Sequential Arrow, Sequential Chevron, Flashing Double Arrow, Flashing Caution, and Alternating Diamond Caution displays. - [Image 2] Page 875 (Figure 6L-3 full page) confirms the same Type C row values (96 x 48 inches, 1 mile, 15 elements) and the three groups of required display modes. - [Image 3] Page 874 shows the Section 6L.06 text, including the Standard that arrow boards shall meet Figure 6L-3 specifications and the required three mode selections. - [Image 4] Page 969 (Notes for Figure 6P-35) concerns mobile operations requiring Type B arrow boards at minimum and does not add Type C specifications. - [Image 5] Page 256 addresses directional arrows on guide signs (Section 2D.08) and is not relevant to arrow board specifications. Citations: - Section 6L.06 Standard §5 (p.833) - Section 6L.06 Standard §16 (p.833) - Section 6L.06 Standard §13 (p.833) - Section 6L.06 Support §6 (p.833) - Figure 6L-3 (p.834) - Page 875 - Page 874

**Gold answer:** Minimum size 96 x 48 inches, minimum legibility distance 1 mile, and at least 15 elements. It must provide at least one directional merge mode, the flashing double-arrow mode, and at least one caution mode.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The downstream end is normally marked by S5-3 END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT | Approaching traffic first encounters an advance School (S1-1) sign, then the School Speed Limit assembly or S5-1 at the beginning of the reduced zone. | S5-2 END SCHOOL ZONE may be used when both zones end together, with a downstream R2-1 optionally mounted. Exact visual hit=0.00, visual recall=0.00, and visual precision=0.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Entering a reduced school speed limit zone, drivers must first encounter a School (S1-1) sign in advance, then a School Speed Limit assembly or School Speed Limit When Flashing (S5-1) sign placed at or near the point where the reduced zone begins. Where the speed reduction exceeds 10 mph, a Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead (S4-5 or S4-5a) sign should also precede the zone, and if used it must be followed by the School Speed Limit sign/assembly. Leaving the zone, the downstream end must be marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign (with limited exceptions). Standards (mandatory): - "A School Speed Limit assembly (see Figure 7B-1) or a School Speed Limit When Flashing (S5-1) sign (see Figure 7B-1) shall be used to indicate the speed limit where a reduced school speed limit zone has been established based upon an engineering study or where a reduced school speed limit is specified for such areas by statute. The School Speed Limit assembly or School Speed Limit When Flashing sign shall be placed at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "If a reduced school speed limit zone has been established, a School (S1-1) sign shall be installed in advance (see Table 2C-3 for advance placement guidelines) of the first School Speed Limit sign assembly or S5-1 sign that is encountered in each direction as traffic approaches the reduced school speed limit zone (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "If used, the Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead sign shall be followed by a School Speed Limit sign or a School Speed Limit assembly." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, the downstream end of an authorized and posted reduced school speed limit zone shall be identified with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign (see Figures 7B-1, 7B-2, and 7B-4)." - Where higher fines apply only to speeding: "a FINES HIGHER (R2-6P), FINES DOUBLE (R2-6aP), or $XX FINE (R2-6bP) plaque shall be posted with the School Speed Limit (S5-1) sign and shall not be posted beneath the School Zone (S1-1) sign." Guidance (recommended): - "A Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead (S4-5 or S4-5a) sign (see Figure 7B-1) should be used to inform road users of a reduced speed zone where the speed limit is being reduced by more than 10 mph, or where engineering judgment indicates that advance notice would be appropriate." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 7B-2 illustrates the full entering/leaving sequence in each direction: a School (S1-1) sign with optional SCHOOL (S4-3P) plaque in advance, a Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead (S4-5) sign, the School Speed Limit assembly (SCHOOL S4-3P plaque, SPEED LIMIT 20 R2-1, and S4-1P time plaque) at the zone beginning, and at the downstream end an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign or END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign, each with an optional SPEED LIMIT 45 (R2-1) sign above. [Image 2] Figure 7B-1 shows the individual school area signs, including the S1-1 School sign, the School Speed Limit assembly variants (S4-3P, R2-1, S4-1P, S4-2P, S4-4P, S4-6P), the S5-1 School Speed Limit When Flashing sign, the S4-5/S4-5a Reduced School Speed Limit Ahead signs, and the S5-2/S5-3 end-of-zone signs. [Image 3] Page 1019 shows the relevant Section 7B.05 Standard text requiring the School Speed Limit assembly at the zone beginning, the advance S1-1 sign, and the S5-3 sign at the downstream end. [Image 4] Page 1024 shows Section 7B.06 material on higher-fines plaques and end-of-zone alternatives in school areas. [Image 5] Page 1016 is the full-page view of Figure 7B-2 showing the sequence in context. [Image 6] Page 1020 shows Figure 7B-4, a comparable school speed-limit zone signing layout with higher-fines plaques posted with the School Speed Limit assembly. Citations: - Section 7B.05 Standard §1 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §2 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §14 (p.980) - Section 7B.05 Standard §3 (p.978) - Section 7B.06 Standard §3 (p.980) - Section 7B.05 Guidance §13 (p.980) - Figure 7B-2 (p.975) - Figure 7B-1 (p.973) - Page 1019 - Page 1024 - Page 1016 - Page 1020

**Gold answer:** Approaching traffic first encounters an advance School (S1-1) sign, then the School Speed Limit assembly or S5-1 at the beginning of the reduced zone. The downstream end is normally marked by S5-3 END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT; S5-2 END SCHOOL ZONE may be used when both zones end together, with a downstream R2-1 optionally mounted.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The Crossbuck is at the top, the inverted-T Number of Tracks plaque is directly below it, and the YIELD or STOP sign is below the plaque on the support. | The Number of Tracks plaque is required when two or more tracks are present.
