**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Reading Figure 8B-2 from top to bottom, the assembly consists of the Crossbuck (R15-1) sign at the top (mounted at approximately 9 feet, which may be varied), the inverted-T Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque immediately below the Crossbuck, and the YIELD (R1-2) or STOP (R1-1) sign below the plaque, with a 2-inch retroreflective strip running down the support to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway. The Number of Tracks plaque is required whenever two or more tracks are present at the grade crossing. Standards (mandatory): - "A Crossbuck Assembly shall consist of a Crossbuck (R15-1) sign, and a Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque if two or more tracks are present, that complies with the provisions of Section 8B.03, and either a YIELD (R1-2) or STOP (R1-1) sign installed on the same support, except as provided in Paragraph 10 of this Section. YIELD or STOP signs used at passive grade crossings shall be installed in compliance with the provisions of Section 2B.18, and Figures 8B-2 and 8B-3." - "If there are two or more tracks at a grade crossing, the number of tracks shall be indicated on a supplemental Number of Tracks (R15-2P) plaque (see Figure 8B-1) of inverted T shape mounted below the Crossbuck sign in the manner shown in Figure 8B-2." - "If a Crossbuck sign support at a passive grade crossing does not include a YIELD or STOP sign (either because the YIELD or STOP sign is placed on a separate support or because a YIELD or STOP sign is not present on the approach), a vertical strip of retroreflective white material, not less than 2 inches in width, shall be used for the full length of the front of the support from the Crossbuck sign or Number of Tracks plaque to within 2 feet above the near edge of the roadway." Guidance (recommended): - The use of STOP signs at passive grade crossings should be limited to unusual conditions where a Diagnostic Team determines a full stop is necessary, considering line of sight, number of tracks, train/vehicle speeds, and crash history. - If the YIELD or STOP sign is on a separate support than the Crossbuck sign, it should be placed in the same plane as the Crossbuck sign and closer to the traveled way, with a minimum 2-inch separation between the nearest points of the signs. Options (permitted): - A strip of retroreflective white material not less than 2 inches in width may be used on the back of each blade of each Crossbuck sign at active grade crossings. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 8B-2 shows the vertical order — RAILROAD CROSSING crossbuck at the top (about 9 ft to the near edge of roadway, variable), the "3 TRACKS" inverted-T plaque directly below, the YIELD or STOP sign below the plaque (mounted at least 4 ft on existing supports; at least 5 ft for new rural installations and 7 ft where parking or pedestrian movements are likely), and a 2-inch retroreflective strip on the support extending to within 2 ft of the roadway edge. [Image 2] Figure 8E-5 shows the same top-to-bottom arrangement (Crossbuck, "2 TRACKS" plaque, YIELD or STOP sign) as applied at a pathway or sidewalk grade crossing. [Image 3] Page 1041 restates the pathway/sidewalk mounting-height and offset details for the same assembly configuration. [Image 5] Page 1038 contains Section 8E.05 with the same figure showing the Crossbuck-over-plaque-over-YIELD/STOP order. [Image 6] Page 1036 (Section 8B.03 provisions) confirms that with two or more tracks the Number of Tracks plaque shall be mounted below the Crossbuck sign as shown in Figure 8B-2. Citations: - Section 8B.04 Standard §1 (p.995) - Section 8B.03 Standard §6 (p.993) - Section 8B.04 Standard §20 (p.1000) - Section 8B.04 Guidance §6 (p.997) - Section 8B.04 Guidance §13 (p.1000) - Section 8B.03 Option §9 (p.995) - Figure 8B-2 (p.993) - Figure 8E-5 (p.1039)

**Gold answer:** The Crossbuck is at the top, the inverted-T Number of Tracks plaque is directly below it, and the YIELD or STOP sign is below the plaque on the support. The Number of Tracks plaque is required when two or more tracks are present.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A bicycle box must be at least 10 feet long (measured from the upstream edge nearest the motor-vehicle stop line to the downstream edge nearest the crosswalk or intersection) and must contain at least one bicycle symbol marking. Turns on red are prohibited from any lane where a bicycle box is placed. Green-colored pavement is optional in a bicycle box, but if it is used it must cover the full limits of the box. Standards (mandatory): - "If used, the distance from the upstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest to the stop line for motor vehicles to the downstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest the crosswalk or intersection shall be at least 10 feet. At least one bicycle symbol marking (see Figure 9E-12) shall be used in the bicycle box." - "Turns on red shall be prohibited from the lane where a bicycle box is placed." (Section 9E.12, shown on the full-page view of the section text [Image 3]). - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be used in the full limits of the bicycle box." (Section 9E.12, shown below Figure 9E-12 Sheet 1 [Images 1, 4]). - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be limited to: … E. Bicycle Boxes (see Section 9E.12) …" — confirming that bicycle boxes are one of the permitted applications of green pavement. Options (permitted): - "A bicycle box (see Figure 9E-12) may be used to increase the visibility of stopped bicycles on the approach to a signalized intersection during the portion of the signal cycle when a red signal indication is being displayed to motor vehicles in the approach lane(s) that is behind the box." - "Green-colored pavement may be used in a bicycle box." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 9E-12 Sheet 1 shows a bicycle box across one lane dimensioned "10 ft MIN." between the advance stop line and the intersection stop line, with bicycle symbol markings inside the optional green-colored pavement. - [Image 2] Figure 9E-12 Sheet 2 shows a bicycle box across multiple lanes with a NO TURN ON RED (R10-11) sign posted, illustrating the turn-on-red prohibition. - [Image 3] The full page of Section 9E.12 text includes the Standard provisions for the 10-foot minimum, the bicycle symbol requirement, and the prohibition of turns on red from the lane where a bicycle box is placed. - [Image 4] This page repeats Figure 9E-12 Sheet 1 and carries the Standard statement that green-colored pavement, if used, shall cover the full limits of the bicycle box. - [Image 5] Section 3H.06 lists Bicycle Boxes (Section 9E.12) among the exclusive permitted applications of green-colored pavement. **Gold answer:** The bicycle box must be at least 10 feet long, include at least one bicycle symbol, prohibit turns on red from its lane, and, if green pavement is used, fill the entire limits of the box.

## T001

**Question:** What is the purpose of the MUTCD, and what four objectives are used to achieve that purpose?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1A.01, Section 1B.01, p.1, p.4, 23 U.S.C. 109(d). Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The MUTCD establishes uniform national criteria for traffic control devices on streets, highways, pedestrian and bicycle facilities, and site roadways open to public travel. | Its objectives are to promote safety, inclusion, and mobility | promote consistency in use, installation, and operation Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.832 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD's purpose is to establish uniform national criteria for the use of traffic control devices that meet the needs and expectancy of road users on all streets, highways, pedestrian and bicycle facilities, and site roadways open to public travel. This purpose is achieved through four objectives: (A) promoting safety, inclusion, and mobility for all road users; (B) promoting efficiency through national uniformity in the meaning and appearance of traffic control devices; (C) promoting national consistency in the use, installation, and operation of devices; and (D) providing basic principles for traffic engineers to use in decisions about the use, installation, operation, maintenance, and removal of devices. Note that these purpose and objective statements are Support (explanatory) material, not normative provisions; however, the MUTCD itself carries mandatory legal status as the national standard for traffic control devices. Standards (mandatory): - The MUTCD "is incorporated by reference in 23 Code of Federal Regulations (CFR), Part 655, Subpart F and shall be recognized as the national standard for all traffic control devices installed on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel... in accordance with 23 U.S.C. 109(d) and 402(a)." Citations: - Section 1A.01 Support §1 (p.1) - Section 1A.01 Support §2 (p.1) - Section 1B.01 Standard §1 (p.4)

**Gold answer:** The MUTCD establishes uniform national criteria for traffic control devices on streets, highways, pedestrian and bicycle facilities, and site roadways open to public travel. Its objectives are to promote safety, inclusion, and mobility; promote efficiency through national uniformity in device meaning and appearance; promote consistency in use, installation, and operation; and provide basic decision-making principles for traffic engineers.

Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1A.01, Section 1B.01, Section 1B.08, Section 1B.05, p.1, p.4. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Applicability is independent of public or private ownership or jurisdiction and independent of whether funding is Federal, State, local, or private. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.877 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The MUTCD's applicability to a facility open to public travel does not depend on whether the facility is publicly or privately owned, nor on its funding source — the Manual explicitly notes that applicability is independent of ownership, jurisdiction, and funding (Federal, State, local, or private). What matters is whether the facility is open to public travel; certain facilities such as fully gated private roadways and parking areas are expressly excluded regardless of ownership. Standards (mandatory): - "In accordance with 23 CFR 655.603(a), the MUTCD shall apply to all of the following types of facilities: A. Any street, roadway, or bikeway open to public travel, either publicly or privately owned; B. Streets and roadways on sites that are off the public right-of-way that are open to public travel without full-time access restrictions... C. Publicly-owned toll roads, including those under the jurisdiction of a public agency, public authority, or public-private partnership; D. Privately-owned toll roads where the public is allowed to travel without access restriction... and E. Grade crossings of publicly-owned roadways with railroads or light rail transit." - "The MUTCD shall not apply to the following types of facilities: A. Roadways within private gated properties where access to the general public is restricted at all times; B. Grade crossings of privately-owned roadways with railroads; and C. Parking areas, including the driving aisles within those parking areas, that are either publicly or privately owned." Guidance (recommended): - An owner of a site roadway open to public travel (or a local jurisdiction or toll facility operator) requesting permission to experiment or to use a device under an interim approval should first check for any State laws, regulations, and/or directives covering the application of the MUTCD provisions that might apply — reflecting that private owners of facilities open to public travel are subject to the Manual just as public agencies are. Citations: - Section 1A.01 Support §5 (p.1) - Section 1B.01 Standard §2 (p.4) - Section 1B.01 Standard §3 (p.4) - Section 1B.08 Guidance §1 (p.12) - Section 1B.05 Guidance §7 (p.7)

**Gold answer:** No. Applicability is independent of public or private ownership or jurisdiction and independent of whether funding is Federal, State, local, or private.

Support (informational, not normative): - "Infrastructure elements that restrict the road user's travel paths or vehicle speeds, such as islands, curbs, speed humps, and other raised roadway surfaces, are not traffic control devices. Transverse or longitudinal rumble strips are also not traffic control devices." Related operational devices (fencing, roadway lighting, barriers, attenuators) are shown in the Manual only for context. - "Although they are not considered to be traffic control devices, raised islands (see Chapter 3J) are also sometimes used to channelize traffic." Citations: - Section 1A.02 Support §2 (p.1) - Section 3I.01 Support §2 (p.632) - Section 6A.03 Standard §2 (p.768) - Section 1B.01 Standard §1 (p.4)

**Gold answer:** No. Infrastructure elements that physically restrict travel paths or speeds, including islands, curbs, speed humps, other raised surfaces, and transverse or longitudinal rumble strips, are not traffic control devices.

Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1C.01, Section 1D.03, Section 6P.01, Section 1B.02, p.13, p.5. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A Standard is mandatory or prohibitive and typically uses 'shall.' Guidance is recommended practice with justified deviations allowed and typically uses 'should.' An Option is per… Adjudication note: Correct Standard, Guidance, and Option distinctions; the full Support definition is not stated. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Section 1C.01 defines the four headings: a Standard is a required, mandatory, or specifically prohibitive practice (typically using "shall"); Guidance is a recommended practice in typical situations (typically using "should") from which deviation is allowed based on engineering judgment or study; and an Option is a permissive condition carrying no requirement or recommendation. The evidence text is truncated before the full Support definition, but other provisions indicate Support material is informational/explanatory in nature, distinct from the normative headings. Standards (mandatory): - "Standard-a statement of required, mandatory, or specifically prohibitive practice regarding a traffic control device. … All Standard statements are labeled, and the text appears in bold type. The verb 'shall' is typically used. The verbs 'should' and 'may' are not used in Standard statements. Standard statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." - "Guidance-a statement of recommended practice in typical situations, with deviations allowed if engineering judgment or engineering study (see Section 1D.03) indicates the deviation to be appropriate. All Guidance statements are labeled, and the text appears in unbold italic type. The verb 'should' is typically used. The verbs 'shall' and 'may' are not used in Guidance statements. Guidance statements are sometimes modified by Option statements." - "Option-a statement of practice that is a permissive condition and carries no requirement or recommendation. Option statements sometimes contain allowable modifications to a Standard or Guidance statement." - "In limited, location-specific cases, the results of a documented engineering study (see Section 1D.03) might indicate a deviation from one or more requirements of a Standard provision to be appropriate." - Agency documents "that change or modify Standard, Guidance, or Option provisions in this Manual shall be considered as supplements to the MUTCD and shall also be revised to be in substantial conformance with the National MUTCD." Note on Support: The supplied excerpt of Section 1C.01 is cut off before the full definition of "Support," so its complete definition and typography cannot be quoted from this evidence. However, Section 6P.01 shows that notes in typical applications are "clearly classified using headings as being Standard, Guidance, Option, or Support," and that unclassified information is generally regarded as Guidance—confirming that Support is a distinct, non-normative category. Citations: - Section 1C.01 Standard §1 (p.13) - Section 1B.02 Standard §3 (p.5) - Section 6P.01 Support §2 (p.857)

**Gold answer:** A Standard is mandatory or prohibitive and typically uses 'shall.' Guidance is recommended practice with justified deviations allowed and typically uses 'should.' An Option is permissive and typically uses 'may.' Support is informational and conveys no mandate, recommendation, permission, prohibition, or enforceable condition.

Standards (mandatory): - "Where the content of this Manual requires a decision for implementation, such decisions shall be made by an engineer, or an individual under the supervision of an engineer... Those decisions shall be made using engineering judgment or engineering study, as required by the MUTCD provision." - For Standard statements: "In limited, location-specific cases, the results of a documented engineering study (see Section 1D.03) might indicate a deviation from one or more requirements of a Standard provision to be appropriate." - For Guidance statements: "a statement of recommended practice in typical situations, with deviations allowed if engineering judgment or engineering study (see Section 1D.03) indicates the deviation to be appropriate." Options (permitted): (none in the evidence) Visual evidence: (none) Support/explanatory context: - Section 1D.03 explains that a deviation from a Standard "is allowed, provided that the agency or official having jurisdiction fully documents, through an engineering study, the engineering basis for the deviation" — reinforcing that documentation is intrinsic to the engineering study, not to engineering judgment. - Section 1D.03 also notes that definitions of professional engineer, engineering study, and engineering judgment are provided in Section 1C.02 (not included in the evidence supplied). - Section 4C.01 illustrates the documentation role of an engineering study in practice: agencies can install an unwarranted signal "but only after conducting an engineering study that documents the rationale." - Section 1A.01 presumes the MUTCD user has professional training and experience in traffic engineering principles when applying study or judgment. Citations: - Section 1A.04 Standard §2 (p.2) - Section 1C.01 Standard §1 (p.13) - Section 1D.03 Support §1 (p.32) - Section 1D.03 Support §2 (p.32) - Section 4C.01 Support §20 (p.653) - Section 1A.01 Support §6 (p.1)

**Gold answer:** Both involve evaluating pertinent information and applying engineering principles by a professional engineer or a person under such an engineer's supervision. Engineering judgment does not require documentation; an engineering study must be documented in writing.

The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Yes — the MUTCD applies to roadways within shopping centers that are open to public travel without full-time access restrictions; these are termed "site roadways open to public travel." However, the MUTCD expressly does not apply to parking areas, including the driving aisles within those parking areas, regardless of whether they are publicly or privately owned. Standards (mandatory): - "In accordance with 23 CFR 655.603(a), the MUTCD shall apply to all of the following types of facilities: ... B. Streets and roadways on sites that are off the public right-of-way that are open to public travel without full-time access restrictions. Examples include roadways within shopping centers, office parks, airports, sports arenas, other similar business and/or recreation facilities, governmental office complexes, schools, universities, recreational parks, and other similar publicly-owned complexes and/or recreation facilities. The above-described examples of streets and roadways are referred to in this Manual as site roadways open to public travel." - "The MUTCD shall not apply to the following types of facilities: ... C. Parking areas, including the driving aisles within those parking areas, that are either publicly or privately owned." Guidance (recommended): - Before requesting permission to experiment with a new device or application, an owner of a site roadway open to public travel should first check for any laws, regulations, and/or directives covering the application of the MUTCD that might apply. Citations: - Section 1B.01 Standard §2 (p.4) - Section 1B.01 Standard §3 (p.4) - Section 1B.05 Guidance §7 (p.7) - Section 1A.01 Support §5 (p.1) - Section 6A.04 Support §1 (p.768) Supporting context (informational, non-normative): The MUTCD's applicability to facilities open to public travel is independent of ownership or jurisdiction (public or private) and funding source, and MUTCD crashworthiness provisions apply to all streets, highways, and site roadways open to public travel — which would include shopping center roadways.

**Gold answer:** It applies to the shopping-center site roadways that are open to public travel without full-time access restrictions. It does not apply to the parking area itself, including its driving aisles.

Substantial conformance of such State or other Federal agency MUTCDs or Supplements shall be as defined in 23 CFR 655.603(b)(1)." - "Policies, directives, specifications, standard drawings, or similar documents that are issued by an agency and that change or modify Standard, Guidance, or Option provisions in this Manual shall be considered as supplements to the MUTCD and shall also be revised to be in substantial conformance with the National MUTCD." - "After the effective date of a new edition of the MUTCD or a revision thereto, or after the adoption thereof by the State, whichever occurs later, new or reconstructed devices installed shall comply with the new edition or revision, as required by 23 CFR 655.603." ## T009

**Question:** When a non-compliant traffic control device is replaced because it is damaged, missing, or no longer serviceable, what is the general rule and the limited replacement-in-kind exception?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 1C.02, Section 1B.03, Section 5A.04, Section 6K.01, Section 1D.10, p.6. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: A non-compliant device may be replaced in kind only when engineering judgment finds that a lone compliant device among adjacent non-compliant devices would confuse users and/or th… | The replacement must generally be a compliant device. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The general rule is that when a non-compliant device is replaced or refurbished because it is damaged, missing, or no longer serviceable, it must be replaced with a compliant device. The limited exception permits replacement in kind, based on engineering judgment, when a single compliant device within a series of adjacent non-compliant devices would confuse road users, and/or when the scheduled replacement of the whole series will still achieve timely MUTCD compliance. Standards (mandatory): - "A non-compliant traffic control device that is being replaced or refurbished because it is damaged, missing, or no longer serviceable (see definition in Section 1C.02) for any reason shall be replaced with a compliant device, except as provided for in Paragraph 13 of this Section." - "Unless a particular device is no longer serviceable (see definition in Section 1C.02), non-compliant devices on existing highways and bikeways shall be brought into compliance with the current edition of the National MUTCD as part of the systematic upgrading of substandard traffic control devices (and installation of new required traffic control devices) required pursuant to the Highway Safety Program, 23 U.S.C. §402(a)." - "Channelizing devices that are no longer serviceable (see definition in Section 1C.02) shall be replaced." Guidance (recommended): - Agencies should establish maintenance policies incorporating effective practices to identify and then fix or replace in a timely manner any traffic control device that is reaching the end of its useful life, or that is damaged or otherwise no longer serviceable. - Functional maintenance of traffic control devices should be used to determine if certain devices need to be changed to meet current traffic conditions. Options (permitted): - A non-compliant traffic control device may be replaced in kind when engineering judgment indicates it is more appropriate because: (A) one compliant device in the midst of a series of adjacent non-compliant devices would be confusing to road users, and/or (B) the schedule for replacement of the whole series of non-compliant devices will result in achieving timely compliance with the MUTCD. Citations: - Section 1B.03 Standard §12 (p.6) - Section 1B.03 Option §13 (p.6) - Section 1B.03 Standard §5 (p.5) - Section 5A.04 Guidance §6 (p.762) - Section 6K.01 Standard §15 (p.815) - Section 1D.10 Guidance §1 (p.38)

**Gold answer:** The replacement must generally be a compliant device. A non-compliant device may be replaced in kind only when engineering judgment finds that a lone compliant device among adjacent non-compliant devices would confuse users and/or the planned replacement schedule for the whole series will achieve timely compliance.

This flows from the broader federal requirement that devices on roads open to public travel be in substantial conformance with FHWA standards. Standards (mandatory): - "A traffic control device or application that does not comply with the provisions of this Manual shall not be used on any street, highway, bikeway, or site roadway open to public travel (see definition in Section 1C.02) without first receiving official approval to experiment from the FHWA's Office of Transportation Operations." - "The U.S. Secretary of Transportation, under authority granted by the Highway Safety Act of 1966, decreed that traffic control devices on all streets and highways open to public travel in accordance with 23 U.S.C. 109(d) and 402(a) in each State shall be in substantial conformance with the Standards issued or endorsed by the FHWA." Options (permitted): - (Support/informational context, not normative:) After an experiment ends, the FHWA Office of Transportation Operations might allow an effective, safety-neutral experimental device to remain in use, typically when it is being actively considered for interim approval under Section 1B.07; interim approval then allows optional use of the device under agreed conditions until rulemaking occurs. Citations: - Section 1B.05 Standard §2 (p.6) - Section 1B.03 Standard §1 (p.5) - Section 1B.03 Support §2 (p.5) - Section 1B.05 Support §15 (p.9) - Section 1B.07 Support §3 (p.11)

**Gold answer:** No. A non-compliant device or application may not be used on a covered public-travel facility without first receiving official experimentation approval from the FHWA Office of Transportation Operations.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Failure to submit two consecutive semi-annual progress reports terminates the experiment and rescinds FHWA approval, requiring restoration of the sites to MUTCD-compliant conditio… | The final report must be submitted within 3 months after the experiment concludes. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The final report for an approved experiment must be submitted within 3 months of the experiment's conclusion; if no final report is received and no mitigating circumstances are reported within 6 months of the end of the approved experimentation period, the experiment is terminated and FHWA's approval is rescinded. Similarly, failure to submit two consecutive semi-annual progress reports terminates the experiment and rescinds approval, and in either case the site(s) must be restored to MUTCD compliance within 3 months. Standards (mandatory): - "The experimenting agency shall submit a final report within 3 months of the conclusion of an approved experiment. If a final report is not received by the FHWA's Office of Transportation Operations, and the experimenting agency fails to notify the FHWA of any mitigating circumstances within 6 months of the end of the approved experimentation period, then the experiment shall be considered terminated and shall constitute rescission of the FHWA's approval to the experimenting agency, requiring restoration of the site(s) to a condition that complies with the provisions of this Manual within 3 months." - "The experimenting agency shall submit a semi-annual progress report for any approved experiment even if no work was performed during the previous reporting period. Failure to submit two consecutive progress reports shall result in termination of the experiment and shall constitute rescission of the FHWA's approval to the experimenting agency, requiring restoration of the site(s) to a condition that complies with the provisions of this Manual within 3 months." - "The required semi-annual progress reports shall be submitted throughout the course of an approved experiment in accordance with the following schedule: A. No later than August 1st for the preceding period of January through June; and B. No later than February 1st for the preceding period of July through December." Citations: - Section 1B.05 Standard §14 (p.9) - Section 1B.05 Standard §13 (p.8) - Section 1B.05 Standard §12 (p.8)

**Gold answer:** The final report must be submitted within 3 months after the experiment concludes. Failure to submit two consecutive semi-annual progress reports terminates the experiment and rescinds FHWA approval, requiring restoration of the sites to MUTCD-compliant conditions within 3 months.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A new traffic control device must be in the public domain to be eligible for official experimentation or interim approval. Express abandonment of all proprietary protections (patents, trademarks, copyrights) satisfies this requirement, and experimentation requests must include a legally-binding certification of public-domain status from the experimenting agency, the manufacturer/developer, and the supplier. Standards (mandatory): - "A traffic control device design or application shall not be eligible for official experimentation (see Section 1B.05) or interim approval (see Section 1B.07) unless it is in the public domain. Express abandonment of any and all forms of proprietary protection, such as patents, trademarks, or copyrights, related to the design and application of the traffic control device shall satisfy the requirement for the traffic control device to be in the public domain." - A request for experimentation involving a new device or new application "shall include from the agency conducting the experiment, the manufacturer and/or developer of the device, and the supplier of the device, a legally-binding statement certifying that the traffic control device is not protected by a patent, trademark, or copyright in accordance with Section 1D.06, and that the traffic control device is in the public domain and can be used freely in traffic control device design and application without infringement or claim of trade secret misappropriation." - "The legally-binding statement shall also state that the agency conducting the experiment, the manufacturer and/or developer of the device, and the supplier of the device are aware that if patent, trademark, or copyright protection is established in the future for the device or application, such action will result in its removal from the MUTCD, cancellation of its interim approval, or cancellation of the authorization for experimentation." - The request for permission to experiment shall contain, among other items, "a legally-binding statement that the experimental device or application is in the public domain, in accordance with Paragraph 16 of this Section." Citations: - Section 1D.06 Standard §2 (p.34) - Section 1B.05 Standard §16 (p.9) - Section 1B.05 Standard §10 (p.8)

**Gold answer:** The traffic-control message, design, or application must be in the public domain and not protected by patent, trademark, or copyright. Legally binding statements are required from the relevant agency/developer/supplier for experimentation. Protection of internal components or manufacturing methods can still be allowed.

**Gold answer:** Fluorescent pink is for incident management, orange is for temporary traffic control, and purple is restricted to vehicles with registered electronic toll collection accounts.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. Traffic control devices and their supports are prohibited from bearing advertising or any other message unrelated to traffic control. This prohibition applies equally to changeable message signs (CMS), their supports, and other equipment. Standards (mandatory): - "Traffic control devices or their supports shall not bear any advertising message or any other message that is not related to traffic control." - "CMS shall display only traffic operational, regulatory, warning, and guidance information except as otherwise provided in this Chapter. Advertising or other messages not related to traffic control shall not be displayed on a CMS or on its supports or other equipment." - "Traffic control devices, public announcements or notices, and other signs or messages within the highway right-of-way shall be placed only as authorized by a public authority or the official having jurisdiction... for the purpose of regulating, warning, or guiding traffic." Citations: - Section 1D.07 Standard §1 (p.34) - Section 2L.02 Standard §1 (p.511) - Section 1D.02 Standard §3 (p.31) - Section 1D.02 Support §6 (p.32)

**Gold answer:** No. They may not bear advertising or any message unrelated to traffic control. Acknowledgment signs, Specific Service signs, and Tourist-Oriented Directional signs are not treated as advertising under this provision.

## T016

**Question:** How does the MUTCD classify regulatory, warning, and guide signs by function?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2A.03, Section 2F.12, Section 2G.10, Section 2A.13, Table 2C-3, Table 2A-2. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations. | Warning signs give notice of a situation that might not be readily apparent. | Guide signs show route designations, destinations, directions, distances, services, points of interest, or other geographical, recreational, or cultural information. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD classifies signs by function into three basic categories. Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations; warning signs give notice of situations that might not be readily apparent; and guide signs provide route, destination, direction, distance, service, and other geographical, recreational, or cultural information. This functional classification is a mandatory (Standard) definition, and it carries through even when messages of different functional types are combined on a single sign panel. Standards (mandatory): - "Signs shall be defined by their function as follows: A. Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations. B. Warning signs give notice of a situation that might not be readily apparent. C. Guide signs show route designations, destinations, directions, distances, services, points of interest, and other geographical, recreational, or cultural information." - "When regulatory messages are incorporated within a guide sign, they shall be on a rectangular panel with a black legend on a white background. When warning messages are incorporated within a guide sign, they shall be on a rectangular panel with a black legend on a yellow background." - "A combination of guide and regulatory signs shall be used in advance of and at the initial entry point and all intermediate entry points from general-purpose lanes or facilities to contiguous, barrier-separated, and buffer-separated preferential lanes where access between the preferential and general-purpose lanes is restricted to designated locations." Guidance (recommended): - Signs should be individually installed on separate posts or mountings except where one sign supplements another (among other listed exceptions), reflecting that signs of different functions are generally displayed separately. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2A-2 shows examples of heights and lateral locations of sign installations, illustrating placement of regulatory (STOP, Speed Limit), warning (Turn with advisory speed plaque), and guide (freeway exit, route assemblies, street name) signs. [Image 2] Figure 2A-3 shows typical locations of regulatory signs (STOP, YIELD) at various intersection configurations. [Image 3] Figure 2A-4 relates Chapters 2B and 2C, corresponding to the regulatory and warning sign classifications. [Image 4] Table 2C-3 provides advance placement guidelines specifically for warning signs, reflecting their distinct function of alerting drivers to conditions not readily apparent. [Image 5] shows the table of contents for Chapter 2C (Warning Signs and Object Markers), organizing warning signs by function. [Image 6] shows the table of contents for regulatory sign sections in Chapter 2B and TTC zone regulatory/warning sign chapters. [Image 7] shows Table 2A-2, Common Uses of Sign Colors, which distinguishes regulatory, warning, and guide signs by their color conventions. [Image 8] shows Figure 2B-23, an example of regulatory and warning signs applied at a two-lane roundabout. Citations: - Section 2A.03 Standard §1 (p.40) - Section 2F.12 Standard §11 (p.392) - Section 2G.10 Standard §12 (p.422) - Section 2A.13 Guidance §6 (p.57) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Figure 2A-3 (p.52) - Figure 2A-4 (p.50) - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Page 23 - Page 9 - Page 85 - Page 162

**Gold answer:** Regulatory signs give notice of traffic laws or regulations. Warning signs give notice of a situation that might not be readily apparent. Guide signs show route designations, destinations, directions, distances, services, points of interest, or other geographical, recreational, or cultural information.

**Grade:** C=0.55, Comp=0.45, F=0.70, Cit=0.45, Strict full credit=No

**Evaluation confidence:** 76%

The official rubric did not award strict full credit because at least one dimension scored below 1.00. Completeness rationale: The audit found incomplete coverage even though the automated clause comparison did not isolate a single missing sentence. Faithfulness rationale: The audit records an unsupported, overstated, or source-inconsistent element. The package retains the official deduction without inventing a new source citation. Citation rationale: The response provides some evidence localization, but the audit judged it incomplete, imprecise, or partly incorrect. The response cites: Section 2A.04, Section 1A.05, Section 2D.04, Section 2A.09, Section 2A.08, Section 2D.55. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Word messages generally read horizontally from left to right. | Place, street, and highway names use initial upper-case letters with lower-case letters Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.832 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In general, word messages on signs must be aligned horizontally and read left to right, and must use the standard wording shown in the Manual and the "Standard Highway Signs" publication. Casing rules vary by sign type: on community wayfinding guide signs, destination lettering must use mixed-case (initial capitals with lower-case letters), while all other word messages on those signs must be in all upper-case letters. For word pavement markings, multi-line messages should read in the direction of travel with the first word nearest the road user. Standards (mandatory): - "Except as otherwise provided in this Manual, all word messages shall be aligned horizontally across a sign, reading left to right." - "Except as provided in Section 2A.04, all word messages shall use standard wording as shown in this Manual and in the 'Standard Highway Signs' publication (see Section 1A.05)." - "The lettering for destinations on community wayfinding guide signs shall be a combination of lower-case letters with initial upper-case letters (see Section 2D.04). All other word messages on community wayfinding guide signs shall be in all upper-case letters." - "Standardization of these designs does not preclude further improvement by minor modifications to the orientation of symbols (see Section 2A.09), width of borders, or layout of word messages, but all shapes and colors shall be as indicated." Guidance (recommended): - "If a pavement marking word message consists of more than one line of information, it should read in the direction of travel. The first word of the message should be nearest to the road user." - "Word messages should be as brief as practical and the lettering should be large enough to provide the necessary legibility distance." Visual evidence: [Image 1] Table 2L-3 shows CMS message examples with all upper-case lettering aligned horizontally, and its note states these examples apply to single-color CMS while multi-color, full-matrix CMS with pixel spacing of 20 mm or less should use upper- and lower-case lettering where appropriate. [Image 2] Figure 9D-5 illustrates guide signing on a shared-use path, and the accompanying text applies the same casing pattern (mixed-case destination lettering, all upper-case for other word messages) to Destination Guide signs for shared-use paths. Citations: - Section 2A.08 Standard §1 (p.45) - Section 2A.08 Standard §2 (p.45) - Section 2D.55 Standard §32 (p.271) - Section 2A.04 Standard §8 (p.40) - Section 3B.21 Guidance §2 (p.575) - Section 2D.55 Guidance §28 (p.271) - Table 2L-3 (p.517) - Figure 9D-5 (p.1082)

**Gold answer:** Word messages generally read horizontally from left to right. Place, street, and highway names use initial upper-case letters with lower-case letters; other legends use upper-case letters unless another MUTCD provision specifies otherwise.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A NEW plaque should remain in place for a period of time determined by engineering judgment, but no longer than 12 months after installation. Strobe lights are prohibited — the MUTCD flatly forbids their use to enhance sign conspicuity. Standards (mandatory): - "Strobe lights shall not be used to enhance the conspicuity of highway signs." Guidance (recommended): - If a NEW plaque is used, it should remain in place for a period of time determined by engineering judgment, but not more than 12 months. - The NEW plaque should be removed no later than 12 months after it was installed. Options (permitted): - A NEW (W16-15P) plaque may be mounted above a regulatory sign when a new regulation takes effect to alert road users to the new traffic regulation, or above an advance warning sign (such as a Signal Ahead sign for a newly-installed traffic control signal) to warn of a new traffic condition. - Based upon engineering judgment, permitted methods to enhance a standard sign's conspicuity include increasing the sign's size, dual signing on the left-hand side of the roadway, adding a solid yellow or fluorescent yellow header panel above a regulatory sign, and adding a NEW plaque above a new regulatory or warning sign for a period of time in accordance with the Guidance provision. Citations: - Section 2A.11 Standard §4 (p.49) - Section 2A.11 Guidance §3 (p.49) - Section 2C.60 Guidance §2 (p.197) - Section 2A.11 Option §1 (p.49) - Section 2C.60 Option §1 (p.197)

**Gold answer:** The NEW plaque should remain only for a period determined by engineering judgment and no more than 12 months. Strobe lights may not be used to enhance highway-sign conspicuity.

The response identifies: Section 6F.02, Section 2A.15, Section 8B.04, Figure 2A-2, Figure 6F-1, Figure 2M-3. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The minimum is 5 feet in rural areas and 7 feet in business, commercial, or residential areas where such activity or obstruction is likely, measured as specified from the near pav… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: In rural areas, roadside signs must be mounted at least 5 feet, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement. In business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely to occur (or where the sign's view might be obstructed), the minimum height is 7 feet, measured to the top of the curb or, absent a curb, to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way. The same 5-foot/7-foot rules apply in temporary traffic control zones and for crossbuck assembly YIELD/STOP signs at grade crossings. Standards (mandatory): - "In rural areas, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 5 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - "In business, commercial, or residential areas where parking, bicyclist, or pedestrian movements are likely to occur, or where the view of the sign might be obstructed, the minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the top of the curb, or in the absence of curb, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way, of signs installed at the side of the road shall be 7 feet (see Figure 2A-2)." - For temporary traffic control: "The minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the pavement, of signs installed at the side of the road in rural areas shall be 5 feet (see Figure 6F-1)." - For temporary traffic control: "The minimum height, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the top of the curb, or in the absence of curb, measured vertically from the bottom of the sign to the elevation of the near edge of the traveled way, of signs installed at the side of the road in business, commercial, or residential areas where parking or pedestrian movements are likely to occur, or where the view of the sign might be obstructed, shall be 7 feet (see Figure 6F-1)." - For grade crossings: the mounting height of a YIELD or STOP sign in a Crossbuck Assembly on a new or separate support "shall be at least 5 feet in rural areas and shall be at least 7 feet in areas where parking or pedestrian movements are likely to occur." Guidance (recommended): - Signs mounted on portable sign supports that do not meet the minimum mounting heights provided in Part 2 should not be used for a duration of more than 3 days (except as provided in Paragraph 12 of Section 6F.02). Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2 illustrates a rural roadside STOP sign with a 5 ft minimum height and 12 ft minimum lateral offset (A), and a roadside sign in a business, commercial, or residential area with a 7 ft minimum height above the curb (C), among other typical installations. - [Image 2] This page (Sect. 6F.02) states the 7-foot requirement for business, commercial, or residential areas in temporary traffic control zones and shows Figure 6F-1 with 5 ft MIN. in rural areas (A, B) and 7 ft MIN. in areas with curb or walkway (C, D). - [Image 3] This full-page view of Figure 2A-2 shows the 5 ft minimum for rural roadside signs (A, B) and the 7 ft minimum for a Speed Limit sign in a business, commercial, or residential area where parking or pedestrian movements are likely to occur (C). - [Image 4] This page contains Section 2A.15 Mounting Height, presenting the 5-foot rural standard (¶4) and the 7-foot standard for business, commercial, or residential areas (¶5). - [Image 5] Figure 2M-3 shows recreational/cultural interest sign assemblies mounted at 7 ft minimum in business, commercial, or residential areas and 5 ft minimum in rural areas, consistent with Section 2A.15. Citations: - Section 2A.15 Standard §4 (p.58) - Section 2A.15 Standard §5 (p.58) - Section 6F.02 Standard §4 (p.790) - Section 6F.02 Standard §5 (p.790) - Section 8B.04 Standard §12 (p.1000) - Section 6F.02 Guidance §11 (p.791) - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Page 831 - Page 92 - Page 99 - Page 566

**Gold answer:** The minimum is 5 feet in rural areas and 7 feet in business, commercial, or residential areas where such activity or obstruction is likely, measured as specified from the near pavement edge or curb.

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2A.15, Section 2G.03, Section 2G.08, Section 2G.10, Figure 2A-2, p.51. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: At least 17 feet, except where nearby structures or special constrained structures qualify for the specific exceptions in Section 2A.15. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 3, which produced MRR=0.333. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.525 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Overhead signs must provide a vertical clearance of not less than 17 feet, measured to the sign, light fixture, or sign bridge, over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders. A lesser clearance is permitted only where the mounting structure or other nearby structures along the roadway have a lesser vertical clearance, and in special cases such as tunnels or double-deck bridges. The 17-foot minimum is echoed in the preferential-lane provisions for signs post-mounted on median barriers. Standards (mandatory): - "Overhead signs shall provide a vertical clearance of not less than 17 feet to the sign, light fixture, or sign bridge over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders, except where the structure on which the overhead signs are to be mounted or other structures along the roadway near the sign structure have a lesser vertical clearance." (Section 2A.15, visible in [Image 2]) - "Signs that are post-mounted on a median barrier that overhang any portion of the traveled way shall be mounted with a vertical clearance that complies with that of overhead signs." (Section 2A.15, visible in [Image 2]) Options (permitted): - "If the vertical clearance of other structures along the roadway near the sign structure is less than 16 feet, the vertical clearance to an overhead sign structure or support may be as low as 1 foot higher than the vertical clearance of the other structures in order to improve the visibility of the overhead signs." (Section 2A.15, visible in [Image 2]) - "In special cases the clearance to overhead signs may be reduced if necessary because of substandard dimensions in tunnels and other major structures such as double-deck bridges." (Section 2A.15, visible in [Image 2]) - Where lateral clearance is limited, Preferential Lane regulatory signs post-mounted on a median barrier that are 72 inches or less in width may be skewed up to 45 degrees or mounted higher, "such that the vertical clearance to the bottom of the sign, light fixture, or structural support, whichever is lowest, is not less than 17 feet above any portion of the pavement and shoulders." - The same 17-foot vertical clearance allowance applies to barrier-mounted warning signs applicable only to a preferential lane and to Preferential Lane guide signs 72 inches or less in width. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2A-2, panel "H – Overhead sign," illustrates the 17 ft MIN vertical clearance from the pavement/shoulder to the bottom of an overhead sign (along with the 6 ft MIN lateral offset for the support). - [Image 2] shows the Section 2A.15 Standard text requiring not less than 17 feet of vertical clearance to the sign, light fixture, or sign bridge over the entire width of the pavement and shoulders, plus the related Options for reduced clearance near lower structures. Citations: - Figure 2A-2 (p.51) - Page 100 - Section 2G.03 Option §14 (p.411) - Section 2G.08 Option §4 (p.419) - Section 2G.10 Option §18 (p.422)

**Gold answer:** At least 17 feet, except where nearby structures or special constrained structures qualify for the specific exceptions in Section 2A.15.

Guidance (recommended): - Regulatory signs, or regulatory panels within guide signs, indicating restrictions on vehicle type and forms of toll payment at a specific toll plaza lane should be installed over the applicable lane, either on the toll plaza canopy or on a separate structure immediately in advance of the canopy, so that each sign is clearly related to an individual toll lane. Options (permitted): - When ramp control signals are used to meter traffic on a freeway or expressway entrance ramp, regulatory signs with legends appropriate to the control may be installed adjacent to the ramp control signal faces. Plaques such as 2-WAY, 3-WAY, or 4-WAY are expressly prohibited with STOP signs. Standards (mandatory): - "At intersections where all approaches are controlled by STOP signs (see Section 2B.12), an ALL-WAY (R1-3P) supplemental plaque (see Figure 2B-1) shall be mounted below each STOP sign. The ALL-WAY plaque shall have a white legend and border on a red background." - "Supplemental plaques with legends such as 2-WAY, 3-WAY, 4-WAY, or other numbers of ways shall not be used with STOP signs." Guidance (recommended): - The TRAFFIC FROM LEFT (RIGHT) DOES NOT STOP (W4-4aP) plaque or ONCOMING TRAFFIC DOES NOT STOP (W4-4bP) plaque should be used at intersections where STOP signs control all but one approach, unless the only non-stopped approach is from a one-way street (this applies to not-all-way stop situations, not the all-way case). Options (permitted): - The CROSS TRAFFIC DOES NOT STOP (W4-4P) plaque may be used with a STOP sign when engineering judgment indicates conditions could cause road users to misinterpret the intersection as an all-way stop. - The W4-4aP or W4-4bP plaque may be used when such messages more accurately describe the traffic controls established at the intersection. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 2B-1 shows the ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque with white legend on a red background alongside the STOP (R1-1) sign and related plaques. Citations: - Section 2B.04 Standard §4 (p.74) - Section 2B.04 Standard §5 (p.74) - Section 2B.04 Guidance §7 (p.74) - Section 2C.66 Guidance §3 (p.198) - Section 2C.66 Option §1 (p.198) - Section 2C.66 Option §2 (p.198) - Figure 2B-1 (p.74)

**Gold answer:** An ALL-WAY (R1-3P) plaque must be mounted below every STOP sign. Numeric-way plaques such as 2-WAY, 3-WAY, or 4-WAY are not permitted with STOP signs.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A YIELD sign is mandatory at the entrance to a roundabout to require entering road users to yield the right-of-way. YIELD signs must control only the approach roadways — they may not be used to control the circulatory roadway itself. Standards (mandatory): - "A YIELD sign shall be used to require road users to yield the right-of-way to other traffic at the entrance to a roundabout. YIELD signs at roundabouts shall be used to control the approach roadways and shall not be used to control the circulatory roadway." Guidance (recommended): - On an approach to a circular intersection controlled by a YIELD sign and at uncontrolled exits, crosswalks should be a minimum of 20 feet from the edge of the circulatory roadway. Options (permitted): - YIELD (word) and YIELD AHEAD (symbol or word) pavement markings may be used on approaches to roundabouts. - At roundabouts where ONE WAY signs have been installed in the central island, Roundabout Circulation (R6-5P) plaques may be placed below the YIELD signs on approaches to supplement the central island signs. - The Vehicular Traffic Warning sign assembly may be installed on an approach to a circular intersection controlled by a YIELD sign where the crosswalk is at least 20 feet in advance of the yield point at the entrance to the circulatory roadway. - The Non-Vehicular Warning sign assembly may likewise be installed on such an approach where the crosswalk is at least 20 feet in advance of the yield point. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 3D-1 shows optional YIELD word markings, yield lines, and crosswalks set back a minimum of 20 feet on the approach roadways to a roundabout, with no yield control within the circulatory roadway. - [Image 2] Shows Section 3D.05, permitting YIELD (word) and YIELD AHEAD pavement markings on approaches to roundabouts. - [Image 3] Shows Section 2B.51, permitting Roundabout Circulation plaques below YIELD signs on approaches to roundabouts. - [Image 4] Shows the Section 2B.10 Standard requiring YIELD signs at roundabout entrances to control approach roadways only, not the circulatory roadway. - [Image 5] Shows Section 3C.09 guidance that crosswalks on YIELD-controlled approaches should be a minimum of 20 feet from the edge of the circulatory roadway. Citations: - Section 2B.10 Standard §5 (p.78) - Section 3C.09 Guidance §3 (p.593) - Section 3D.05 Option §1 (p.605) - Section 2B.51 Option §4 (p.118) - Section 2C.54 Option §11 (p.192) - Section 2C.55 Option §6 (p.193) - Figure 3D-1 (p.596)

**Gold answer:** A YIELD sign must control each entering approach roadway. It must not be used to control the circulatory roadway.

If the major-street 85th-percentile speed exceeds 40 mph, the minimums may be reduced to 70 percent of those values.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A Speed Limit (R2-1) sign must display the limit established by law, ordinance, regulation, or as adopted by the authorized agency based on an engineering study, and the displayed limits must be in multiples of 5 mph. A Speed Limit sign should not be used to show an advisory speed for a roadway condition; instead, an Advisory Speed plaque mounted below a warning sign is the recommended (and, in specific cases, optional) tool for advisory speeds. Standards (mandatory): - "The Speed Limit (R2-1) sign (see Figure 2B-3) shall display the limit established by law, ordinance, regulation, or as adopted by the authorized agency based on an engineering study. The speed limits displayed shall be in multiples of 5 mph." - For variable speed limit signs on freeways and expressways: "The regulatory speed displayed on a variable Speed Limit sign shall comply with Paragraph 13" of Section 2B.21. Guidance (recommended): - Per the guidance visible on the page containing Section 2B.21: an advisory speed plaque (see Section 2C.59) mounted below a warning sign should be used to warn road users of an advisory speed for a roadway condition; a Speed Limit sign should not be used for this purpose. Options (permitted): - "An Advisory Speed plaque (see Figure 2C-1 and Section 2C.59) may be used when the change in roadway condition requires a reduced speed." - "An Advisory Exit Speed or Advisory Ramp Speed sign may be used based on engineering judgment when the difference between the mainline roadway speed limit and the exit or ramp advisory speed in the vicinity of the departure is 10 mph or less." - "A Speed Limit Sign Beacon may be used with a fixed or variable Speed Limit sign," and a flashing Speed Limit Sign Beacon (with an appropriate accompanying sign) may be used to indicate that the displayed speed limit is in effect. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 2B-3 illustrates the R2-1 Speed Limit sign ("SPEED LIMIT 50") and related speed limit signs and plaques, all showing speeds in multiples of 5 mph (50, 40, 45, 55, 30). - [Image 2] Shows Figure 2C-2, where advisory speeds for a horizontal curve are conveyed by W13-1P Advisory Speed plaques ("35 MPH") mounted below curve warning signs — not by Speed Limit signs. - [Image 3] Shows the full text of Section 2B.21, including Standard §13 (limit established by law/engineering study, multiples of 5 mph) and the guidance that a Speed Limit sign should not be used to warn of an advisory speed for a roadway condition. - [Image 4] Full-page view of Figure 2B-3 confirming the layout and designations (R2-1 through R2-14) of Speed Limit signs and plaques. - [Image 5] Shows a Speed Limit 35 sign with red/orange flags as an example of enhanced conspicuity, consistent with the R2-1 sign displaying a regulatory limit in a multiple of 5 mph. Citations: - Section 2B.21 Standard §13 (p.85) - Section 2G.26 Standard §3 (p.457) - Section 2C.28 Option §2 (p.173) - Section 2C.12 Option §5 (p.160) - Section 4S.04 Option §3 (p.753) - Figure 2B-3 (p.86) - Page 126

**Gold answer:** It must display the speed limit established by law, ordinance, regulation, or an authorized agency based on an engineering study, in multiples of 5 mph. It should not be used for an advisory speed; an advisory speed plaque below an appropriate warning sign should be used instead.

The package retains the official deduction without inventing a new source citation. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 2C.04, Section 6H.02, Section 6B.04, Table 2C-3, Table 6B-1, Figure 2C-2. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Warning signs should be placed to provide adequate perception-response time. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 4, which produced MRR=0.250. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.501 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Advance placement of warning signs should be determined by providing drivers adequate Perception-Response Time (PRT), using the distances in Table 2C-3 (based on posted or 85th-percentile speed and the condition being warned of) applied with engineering judgment. In temporary traffic control zones, signs should be placed in advance of the transition and activity areas at distances that vary with roadway type, condition, and speed—per Table 6B-1—ranging from roughly 100 feet on low-speed urban streets to 1,000 feet or more (up to ½ mile or beyond) on freeways and expressways. These are recommended (Guidance) practices, not mandatory requirements. Guidance (recommended): - Warning signs should be placed so that they provide an adequate PRT; the distances in Table 2C-3 should be applied with engineering judgment, and the effectiveness of placement should be periodically evaluated under both day and night conditions. - Table 2C-3 provides advance placement distances by posted or 85th-percentile speed for Condition A (speed reduction and lane changing in heavy traffic) and Condition B (deceleration to a listed advisory speed); for signs with less than 6-inch legend or more than four words, a minimum of 100 feet should be added to provide adequate legibility. - In TTC zones, where highway conditions permit, warning signs should be placed in advance of the transition and activity areas at varying distances depending on roadway type, condition, and posted speed; where a series of two or more advance warning signs is used, the closest sign should be placed approximately 100 feet (low-speed urban streets) to 1,000 feet or more (freeways and expressways). - On freeways and expressways, advance warning sign placement should extend as far as ½ mile or more because drivers are conditioned to uninterrupted flow. - On urban streets, the effective placement of the nearest warning sign to the TTC zone should range from 4 to 8 times the speed limit in mph, with the high end used when speeds are relatively high; with two or more signs on higher-speed streets, the advance warning area should extend a greater distance (see Table 6B-1). - On rural highways, the effective placement of the first warning sign should be substantially longer—8 to 12 times the speed limit in mph—and the advance warning area should extend 1,500 feet or more for open highway conditions (see Table 6B-1). Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Table 2C-3 gives advance placement distances by speed (20–85 mph) for Condition A and Condition B (advisory speeds 0–80 mph), with footnotes on legibility adjustments and minimum 100-foot spacing. - [Image 2] Table 6B-1 shows warning sign minimum spacing dimensions A, B, and C by road type: 100 feet (urban low speed), 350 feet (urban high speed), 500 feet (rural), and 1,000/1,500/2,640 feet (expressway/freeway). - [Image 3] Page 193 shows Section 2C.04 (Placement of Warning Signs) with its Guidance paragraphs on PRT and Table 2C-3. - [Image 4] Page 839 shows Section 6H.02, Position of Advance Warning Signs, with the guidance on placement distances referencing Table 6B-1. - [Image 5] Page 197 shows Figure 2C-2 (Examples of Warning Signs for Changes in Horizontal Alignment), whose notes direct users to Table 2C-3 for advance placement distance guidelines. - [Image 6] Page 812 shows Section 6B.04 (Advance Warning Area) guidance on the 4–8 and 8–12 times-speed-limit rules and freeway/rural placement distances. Citations: - Section 2C.04 Guidance §5 (p.152) - Section 2C.04 Guidance §804 (p.153) - Section 6H.02 Guidance §1 (p.798) - Section 6B.04 Guidance §3 (p.771) - Section 6B.04 Guidance §4 (p.771) - Section 6B.04 Guidance §6 (p.771) - Table 2C-3 (p.152) - Table 6B-1 (p.773) - Page 193 - Page 839 - Page 197 - Page 812

**Gold answer:** Warning signs should be placed to provide adequate perception-response time. Table 2C-3 is an aid, but its distances should be applied with engineering judgment and can be adjusted for roadway features, other signing, and visibility.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A unit of information is a single answer to a single question that a driver can use to make a decision (e.g., "What happened?" → MAJOR CRASH). The MUTCD recommends that a unit of information contain no more than four words, and it limits how many units may appear in a phase, in a message, and on a line of the CMS. Guidance (recommended): - "A unit of information, which is a single answer to a single question that a driver can use to make a decision, should not be more than four words." - "The maximum length of a message should be dictated by the number of units of information contained in the message, in addition to the size of the CMS." - "No more than three units of information should be displayed in a message phase"; "No more than four units of information should be in a message when the traffic operating speeds are 35 mph or more"; "No more than five units of information should be in a message when the traffic operating speeds are less than 35 mph"; and "Only one unit of information should appear on each line of the CMS." - Display timing should be based on "1 second per word or 2 seconds per unit of information, whichever produces a lesser value," with a phase display time never less than 2 seconds. Options (permitted): - "A unit of information consisting of more than one word may be displayed on more than one line. An additional CMS at a downstream location may be used for the purpose of allowing the entire message to be read twice." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2L-2, which illustrates a message of four units of information—each answering a driver's question (What happened? MAJOR CRASH; Where? AT EXIT 12; Who is the advisory for? Drivers heading TO NEW YORK; What is advised? USE ROUTE 46)—arranged into an example two-phase message. [Image 5] shows the Section 2L.05 message design principles (units per phase, per message, and per line) on page 516. Citations: - Section 2L.05 Guidance §1 (p.514) - Section 2L.05 Guidance §7 (p.516) - Section 2L.05 Support §2 (p.515) - Section 2L.05 Option §9 (p.516) - Section 2L.05 Support §3 (p.515) - Table 2L-2 (p.515)

**Gold answer:** A unit of information is one answer to one question that a driver can use to make a decision. It should contain no more than four words; total message length should be governed by the number of information units and the size of the CMS.

The response identifies: Section 3B.04, Section 3B.05, Section 3B.01, Section 3B.06, Section 9E.13, Figure 3B-1. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Yellow center lines separate lanes traveling in opposite directions. | White lane lines separate lanes traveling in the same direction. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.951 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The color of a longitudinal line communicates the directional relationship of the traffic it separates. Yellow center line markings delineate the separation of traffic lanes that have opposite directions of travel, while white lane line markings delineate the separation of traffic lanes that have the same direction of travel. In short, yellow means opposing traffic is on the other side of the line; white means traffic on both sides is moving the same way. Standards (mandatory): - "Center line pavement markings, when used, shall be the pavement markings used to delineate the separation of traffic lanes that have opposite directions of travel on a roadway and shall be yellow." - "When used, lane line pavement markings delineating the separation of traffic lanes that have the same direction of travel shall be white." - On two-lane, two-way roadways, the yellow center line markings shall be one of: (A) two-direction passing zone markings (normal width broken yellow line, passing permitted in either direction); (B) one-direction no-passing zone markings (double yellow line, one broken and one solid, passing permitted only for traffic adjacent to the broken line); or (C) two-direction no-passing zone markings (two normal width solid yellow lines, passing prohibited in either direction), as shown in Figure 3B-1. - "Except where a reversible lane (see Section 3B.04) or a two-way left-turn lane (see Section 3B.05) is present, the center line markings on undivided two-way roadways with four or more lanes for moving motor vehicle traffic always available shall be the two-direction no-passing zone markings consisting of normal width double solid yellow lines as shown in Figure 3B-2." Options (permitted): - Solid white lane line markings may be used: on approaches to intersections to separate contiguous through lanes; to separate through traffic lanes from auxiliary lanes (such as an added uphill truck lane); and on approaches to crosswalks across multi-lane roadways. - On shared-use paths wide enough for two minimum-width lanes, a solid yellow center line may be used to separate the two directions of travel where passing/traveling left of the line is not permitted, and a broken yellow center line may be used where passing is permitted. Visual evidence: [Image 1] Figure 3B-2 shows a multi-lane two-way roadway where the double solid yellow center line separates the opposing directions (black arrows pointing opposite ways) while broken white lane lines separate same-direction lanes. [Image 2] Figure 3B-3 shows three-lane, two-way marking where the yellow center line (broken-plus-solid or double solid) divides opposing flows and a broken white line divides the two same-direction lanes. [Image 3] Figure 3B-1 shows two-lane, two-way applications with broken and solid yellow center lines defining passing and no-passing zones between opposing directions. [Image 4] Page 541 presents the full Figure 3B-2 with double yellow center lines between opposing multi-lane flows and white lane lines within each direction. [Image 5] Page 542 presents the full Figure 3B-3 illustrating one-direction and two-direction no-passing yellow center lines on a three-lane, two-way roadway. [Image 6] Page 595 shows a one-direction freeway/expressway where all lane separations for same-direction traffic are white (dotted/solid lane lines at a lane drop), with yellow appearing only as the left edge line. [Image 7] Page 674 shows tubular markers supplementing a double yellow center line (separating opposing directions) and white lane lines (separating same-direction lanes) at an unsignalized crosswalk. Citations: - Section 3B.01 Standard §1 (p.540) - Section 3B.06 Standard §1 (p.546) - Section 3B.01 Standard §4 (p.540) - Section 3B.01 Standard §6 (p.540) - Section 3B.06 Option §8 (p.548) - Section 9E.13 Option §1 (p.1106) - Figure 3B-1 (p.540) - Figure 3B-2 (p.541) - Figure 3B-3 (p.542) - Page 582 - Page 583 - Page 595 - Page 674

**Gold answer:** Yellow center lines separate lanes traveling in opposite directions. White lane lines separate lanes traveling in the same direction.
