Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 3B.12, Section 3B.13, Section 8C.02, Section 3B.01, Section 3B.03, Section 2B.36. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: If successive no-passing zones are less than 400 feet apart, the markings should connect the zones. | They are required on roadways with center line markings where passing sight distance is below the minimum in Table 3B-1 for the relevant speed. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: On roadways with center line markings, no-passing-zone markings are mandatory at horizontal or vertical curves where passing sight distance falls below the minimum in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit. On two-way, two-lane or three-lane roadways with center lines, no-passing zones must also be established at curves and other locations where an engineering study shows passing must be prohibited. Where successive no-passing zones are less than 400 feet apart, it is recommended that the markings connect the zones. Standards (mandatory): - "On roadways with center line markings, no-passing zone markings shall be used at horizontal or vertical curves where the passing sight distance is less than the minimum shown in Table 3B-1 for the 85th-percentile speed or the speed limit." - "On two-way, two-lane or three-lane roadways where center line markings are installed, no-passing zones shall be established at vertical and horizontal curves and other locations where an engineering study indicates that passing must be prohibited because of inadequate sight distances or other special conditions." - "No-passing zone markings shall be used on: A. Two-way roadways at lane-reduction transitions (see Section 3B.12), B. Approaches to obstructions that must be passed on the right (see Section 3B.13), C. Approaches to grade crossings (see Section 8C.02), and D. Approaches to crosswalks." - "No-passing zones shall be marked by either the one-direction no-passing zone pavement markings or the two-direction no-passing zone pavement markings described in Section 3B.01 and shown in Figures 3B-1 and 3B-3." Guidance (recommended): - Where the distance between successive no-passing zones is less than 400 feet, no-passing zone markings should connect the zones. Citations: - Section 3B.03 Standard §4 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §3 (p.543) - Section 3B.03 Standard §2 (p.542) - Section 3B.03 Standard §1 (p.542) - Section 3B.03 Guidance §7 (p.543) - Section 2B.36 Support §3 (p.102)

**Gold answer:** They are required on roadways with center line markings where passing sight distance is below the minimum in Table 3B-1 for the relevant speed. If successive no-passing zones are less than 400 feet apart, the markings should connect the zones.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Crosswalk markings must be white, and transverse crosswalk lines must be between 6 and 24 inches wide. The marked crosswalk itself must be at least 6 feet wide (8 feet at a non-intersection crosswalk where the posted speed limit is 40 mph or greater). Figure 3C-1 shows the additional dimensional limits for high-visibility designs (longitudinal bar, ladder, and bar pair). Standards (mandatory): - "The transverse lines used to establish the limits of the ladder crosswalk shall not be less than 6 inches or greater than 24 inches in width." - Per Section 3C.03 (shown on the attached page): "Crosswalk markings shall be white. When used, transverse lines shall not be less than 6 inches or greater than 24 inches in width." - Per Section 3C.03 (shown on the attached page): "Except as provided in Paragraph 6 of this Section, the minimum width of a marked crosswalk shall be 6 feet," and "At a non-intersection crosswalk where the posted speed limit is 40 mph or greater, the minimum width of the crosswalk shall be 8 feet." Guidance (recommended): - Transverse line crosswalk markings should be limited to locations controlled by traffic control signals or on approaches controlled by STOP or YIELD signs. - To provide contrast with aesthetic surface treatments, a gap of at least one-half the width of the white transverse line, but not less than 6 inches, should be used between the white crosswalk lines and the aesthetic surface treatment (such as unmarked pavement or a black contrast line). Options (permitted): - High-visibility crosswalk markings may be used where additional conspicuity is desired for a crosswalk over transverse line crosswalk markings. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 3C-1 shows dimensional limits: transverse lines 6–24 inches wide with a 6 ft minimum crosswalk width; longitudinal bars 12–24 inches wide with gaps of 12–60 inches (not to exceed 2.5 times the bar width); ladder transverse lines 6–24 inches; and bar pairs of 8–12 inch bars with a coupling gap d where 8 inches ≤ d ≤ 12 inches, all with a 6 ft minimum width (8 ft where posted speed is 40 mph or greater at a non-intersection crosswalk). - [Image 2] Page 631 contains Section 3C.03, which states the mandatory white color, the 6–24 inch transverse line width limits, and the 6 ft/8 ft minimum crosswalk widths, along with Figure 3C-1. - [Image 3] Page 632 shows Sections 3C.04 and 3C.05, confirming that transverse line crosswalks consist of two parallel transverse lines and that high-visibility designs include longitudinal bar, ladder, and bar pair designs. - [Image 4] Page 17 is a table of contents listing Chapter 3C sections on crosswalk markings; it provides no dimensional or color requirements. - [Image 5] Page 664 (Section 3H.03/3H.04) shows the contrast-gap guidance for aesthetic treatments relative to white transverse crosswalk lines and Figure 3H-1 examples. Citations: - Section 3C.07 Standard §4 (p.592) - Section 3C.04 Guidance §1 (p.591) - Section 3H.03 Guidance §11 (p.623) - Section 3C.05 Option §1 (p.591) - Figure 3C-1 (p.590) - Page 631 - Page 632 - Page 664

**Gold answer:** Crosswalk markings must be white. Where transverse lines are used, each line must be at least 6 inches and no more than 24 inches wide. Except for certain ramp-terminal cases, the marked crosswalk should be at least 6 feet wide.

## T035

**Question:** Does satisfying a traffic signal warrant automatically require installation of a traffic control signal?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=0.80, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=No

**Descriptive mean:** 95.0% (Strong partial credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 76%

**Rationale:** The response received 95.0% as the descriptive mean of the four official item scores. The official rubric did not award strict full credit because at least one dimension scored below 1.00. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The response omits or underdevelops these locked elements: it is not conclusive justification. | A warrant is a threshold that triggers analysis Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4C.01, Section 2B.12, Section 4B.03, p.651, p.79, p.649. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Installation must be based on an engineering study considering safety, operation, alternatives, and the site. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.933 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: No. The MUTCD explicitly states that satisfying a signal warrant does not by itself require installing a traffic control signal. Warrant satisfaction is only one factor in an engineering study, which must weigh the safe and efficient movement of all road users, and alternatives to signalization should be considered even when warrants are met. Standards (mandatory): - "The satisfaction of a traffic signal warrant or warrants shall not in itself require the installation of a traffic control signal." - Analogously for stop control: "The satisfaction of an all-way stop control warrant or warrants shall not in itself require the installation of all-way stop control at an unsignalized intersection." Guidance (recommended): - Because road user delay and the frequency of some types of crashes can be higher under signal control than under STOP sign control, consideration should be given to providing alternatives to traffic control signals even if one or more signal warrants has been satisfied. - Where a signal is installed based on projected (estimated) volumes, an engineering study should be done within 1 year of putting the signal into steady (stop-and-go) operation to determine if the signal is justified (except where Warrant 8 was used); if not justified, the signal should be taken out of steady operation or removed. Citations: - Section 4C.01 Standard §3 (p.651) - Section 2B.12 Standard §5 (p.79) - Section 4B.03 Guidance §1 (p.649) - Section 4C.01 Guidance §12 (p.652) - Section 4C.01 Support §20 (p.653)

**Gold answer:** No. A warrant is a threshold that triggers analysis; it is not conclusive justification. Installation must be based on an engineering study considering safety, operation, alternatives, and the site. A signal can also be installed where no warrant is met if an engineering study documents why it is the best solution.

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4A.03, Section 4F.01, Section 4F.02, Section 4F.09, p.644, p.682. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully in an associated crosswalk, vehicles lawfully in the intersection, and for left/U-turns, opposing vehicles close enough to be an imme… | The driver may generally proceed straight, turn right or left, or make a U-turn unless another control modifies the movement. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A driver facing a steady CIRCULAR GREEN indication may proceed straight through, turn right or left, or make a U-turn, unless the movement is modified by signs, markings, roadway design, separate turn signals, or other traffic control devices. The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully within an associated crosswalk and to other vehicles lawfully within the intersection; additionally, drivers turning left or making a U-turn to the left must yield to oncoming vehicles that are close enough to constitute an immediate hazard. Standards (mandatory): - "Vehicular traffic facing a CIRCULAR GREEN signal indication is permitted to proceed straight through or turn right or left or make a U-turn movement except as such movement is modified by lane-use signs, turn prohibition signs, lane markings, roadway design, separate turn signal indications, or other traffic control devices." - "Such vehicular traffic, including vehicles turning right or left or making a U-turn movement, shall yield the right-of-way to: (a) Pedestrians lawfully within an associated crosswalk, and (b) Other vehicles lawfully within the intersection." - "In addition, vehicular traffic turning left or making a U-turn movement to the left shall yield the right-of-way to other vehicles approaching from the opposite direction so closely as to constitute an immediate hazard during the time when such turning vehicle is moving across or within the intersection." Options (permitted): - A steady straight-through GREEN ARROW indication may be used instead of a CIRCULAR GREEN indication to discourage wrong-way turns on approaches intersecting a one-way street or an interchange exit ramp, on approaches with unique geometric design prohibiting turns, or on approaches with pre-signals where adjacent lanes are controlled separately. Citations: - Section 4A.03 Standard §1 (p.644) - Section 4F.01 Option §5 (p.682) - Section 4F.02 Support §2 (p.683) - Section 4F.09 Support §2 (p.693)

**Gold answer:** The driver may generally proceed straight, turn right or left, or make a U-turn unless another control modifies the movement. The driver must yield to pedestrians lawfully in an associated crosswalk, vehicles lawfully in the intersection, and for left/U-turns, opposing vehicles close enough to be an immediate hazard.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The exclusive function of the yellow change interval is to warn approaching traffic that their permission to proceed is being terminated, after which they will be directed to stop (or, for a protected/permissive turn, that the protected movement is ending). Its duration must be determined using engineering practices; for hybrid beacons, a range of 3 to 6 seconds is recommended. The evidence provided contains no provision addressing whether a pre-yellow vehicular countdown display may be used, so that part of the question cannot be answered from this evidence. Standards (mandatory): - "The duration of the yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices." - For pedestrian hybrid beacons: "The duration of the steady yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices in accordance with the provisions in Section 4F.17." - For emergency-vehicle hybrid beacons: "The duration of the steady yellow change interval shall be determined using engineering practices in accordance with the provisions in Section 4F.17." Guidance (recommended): - For emergency-vehicle hybrid beacons, a yellow change interval should have a minimum duration of 3 seconds and a maximum duration of 6 seconds, with the longer intervals reserved for approaches with higher speeds. Citations: - Section 4F.17 Support §1 (p.708) - Section 4F.17 Standard §3 (p.708) - Section 4H.11 Support §3 (p.717) - Section 4J.03 Standard §10 (p.730) - Section 4N.03 Standard §4 (p.743) - Section 4N.03 Guidance §5 (p.743) Note on function (Support, informational): The MUTCD explains that the yellow change interval's exclusive purpose is warning traffic that permission to proceed is terminating; providing clearance time to travel through the intersection or conflict area is the purpose of the red clearance interval, not the yellow change interval. Note on the pre-yellow countdown question: The supplied evidence is insufficient to determine whether a pre-yellow vehicular countdown display is permitted or prohibited.

**Gold answer:** The yellow change interval warns that permission to proceed or a protected movement is ending. Its duration must be determined using engineering practices, with guidance of 3 to 6 seconds. Pre-yellow displays such as vehicular countdowns or flashing green indications are prohibited, except for permitted advance-warning beacons.

## T038

**Question:** What walking speed is generally used to calculate pedestrian clearance time, and what alternative speed may be used with an extended push-button feature?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4I.06, Section 4K.05, p.724, p.725, p.734. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The general walking speed is 3.5 feet per second. | Up to 4 feet per second may be used to evaluate sufficiency where an extended push-button press provides slower pedestrians a longer clearance time. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Pedestrian clearance time is generally calculated using a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second, measured from the curb or edge of pavement to the far side of the traveled way or a sufficient median. Where an extended push button press feature is installed to give slower pedestrians extra time, a walking speed of up to 4 feet per second may be used instead. A slower speed than 3.5 feet per second should be considered where slower pedestrians or wheelchair users routinely cross. Guidance (recommended): - "Except as provided in Paragraph 8 of this Section, the pedestrian clearance time should be sufficient to allow a pedestrian crossing in the crosswalk who left the curb or edge of pavement at the end of the WALKING PERSON (symbolizing WALK) signal indication to travel at a walking speed of 3.5 feet per second to at least the far side of the traveled way or to a median of sufficient width for pedestrians to wait." - Where pedestrians who walk slower than 3.5 feet per second, or pedestrians who use wheelchairs, routinely use the crosswalk, a walking speed of less than 3.5 feet per second should be considered in determining the pedestrian clearance time. - The total of the walk interval and pedestrian clearance time should be sufficient to allow a pedestrian who left the pedestrian detector (or a location 6 feet behind the face of the curb or edge of pavement) at the beginning of the WALK indication to travel at 3 feet per second to the far side of the traveled way (or to the median in a two-stage crossing); any additional time required should be added to the walk interval. Options (permitted): - "A walking speed of up to 4 feet per second may be used to evaluate the sufficiency of the pedestrian clearance time at locations where an extended push button press function has been installed to provide slower pedestrians an opportunity to request and receive a longer pedestrian clearance time." Passive pedestrian detection may also be used to automatically adjust clearance time based on actual walking speed or actual clearance of the crosswalk. - The additional time provided by an extended push button press to satisfy pedestrian clearance time needs may be added to either the walk interval or the pedestrian change interval. - Pedestrians may be provided with additional features such as increased crossing time, audible beaconing, or a speech push button information message as a result of an extended push button press. Citations: - Section 4I.06 Guidance §7 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Option §8 (p.724) - Section 4I.06 Option §9 (p.725) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §10 (p.725) - Section 4I.06 Guidance §14 (p.725) - Section 4K.05 Option §1 (p.734)

**Gold answer:** The general walking speed is 3.5 feet per second. Up to 4 feet per second may be used to evaluate sufficiency where an extended push-button press provides slower pedestrians a longer clearance time.

## T039

**Question:** When is a countdown display mandatory on a pedestrian signal head?

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4I.04, Section 9E.12, p.720, p.1105. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: It is mandatory at a crosswalk when the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.667. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A countdown display is mandatory on all pedestrian signal heads at crosswalks where the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds. It is also mandatory where a bicycle box extends across more than one motor vehicle approach lane, in which case the countdown must display without actuation. When the pedestrian change interval is 7 seconds or less, a countdown display is optional. Standards (mandatory): - "All pedestrian signal heads used at crosswalks where the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds shall include a pedestrian change interval countdown display in order to inform pedestrians of the number of seconds remaining in the pedestrian change interval." - "The countdown pedestrian signal shall display the number of seconds remaining until the termination of the pedestrian change interval (flashing UPRAISED HAND). Countdown displays shall not be used during the walk interval. Countdown displays shall not be used during the red clearance interval of a concurrent vehicular phase that is ending simultaneously with or after the end of the pedestrian phase." - "Countdown pedestrian signals shall consist of Portland orange numbers that are at least 6 inches in height on a black opaque background. The countdown pedestrian signal shall be located immediately adjacent to the associated UPRAISED HAND (symbolizing DONT WALK) pedestrian signal head indication." - "Countdown pedestrian signals (see Section 4I.04) for the crosswalk or pedestrian crossing movement that crosses the approach shall accompany bicycle boxes that extend across more than one approach lane for motor vehicles. Countdown pedestrian signals used with bicycle boxes shall display the pedestrian change interval countdown without the need for actuation." Options (permitted): - Pedestrian signal heads used at crosswalks where the pedestrian change interval is 7 seconds or less may include a pedestrian change interval countdown display. Citations: - Section 4I.04 Standard §1 (p.720) - Section 4I.04 Standard §6 (p.720) - Section 4I.04 Standard §4 (p.720) - Section 9E.12 Standard §8 (p.1105) - Section 4I.04 Option §2 (p.720)

**Gold answer:** It is mandatory at a crosswalk when the pedestrian change interval is more than 7 seconds.

Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 4J.03, Section 4J.02, Figure 4J-3, Page 769, Page 770, Page 771. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Flashing circular yellow, then steady circular yellow, then both steady circular red during the walk interval, then alternating flashing circular red during the pedestrian change… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.975 and Context Precision@6=0.833. The visual-routing decision did not match the locked modality requirement for this question. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: After a pedestrian actuation, the beacon must display a flashing circular yellow, then a steady circular yellow, then both steady circular red indications during the pedestrian walk interval, then alternating flashing circular red indications during the pedestrian change interval, and finally revert to dark. Between actuations the beacon must remain dark. An optional steady red clearance interval may follow the steady yellow, and an optional flashing-red buffer interval may follow the pedestrian change interval. Standards (mandatory): - "Following an actuation by a pedestrian, a pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall display a flashing CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by a steady CIRCULAR yellow signal indication, followed by both steady CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian walk interval, followed by alternating flashing CIRCULAR RED signal indications during the pedestrian change interval (see Figure 4J-3). Upon … termination of the pedestrian change interval, the pedestrian hybrid beacon faces shall revert to a dark (not illuminated) condition." - "Pedestrian hybrid beacon indications shall be dark (not illuminated) during periods between actuations." - "A pedestrian hybrid beacon face shall consist of three signal sections, with a CIRCULAR YELLOW signal indication centered below two horizontally-aligned CIRCULAR RED signal indications (see Figure 4J-3)." Guidance (recommended): - If the pedestrian hybrid beacon is coordinated as part of a signal system, it should remain in the dark condition after a pedestrian actuation has been received until the point in the background cycle when the predetermined duration of the flashing yellow interval needs to be initiated to achieve the appropriate coordinated offset. Options (permitted): - If a minimum dark time between activations has been set on the controller, the beacon may remain in the dark condition after a pedestrian actuation until the minimum dark time has been provided. - Per the note to Figure 4J-3, an optional steady red clearance interval may be added after Interval 3 (steady yellow), and an optional short buffer interval (alternating flashing red while the pedestrian signal heads display a steady UPRAISED HAND) may be added after Interval 5. Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 4J-3 illustrates the six-step sequence: (1) dark until activated, (2) flashing yellow upon activation, (3) steady yellow, (4) steady red during the pedestrian walk interval, (5) alternating flashing red during the pedestrian change interval, and (6) dark again until activated, with a note allowing optional steady red clearance and buffer intervals. - [Image 4] Page 769 shows Figure 4J-3 in page context with the sequence diagrams and legend (SY, FY, SR, FR) above Section 4J text. - [Image 2] Page 770 contains the Section 4J.03 Standard text stating the required post-actuation sequence and reversion to dark. - [Image 3] Page 771 continues Section 4J.03, including the corresponding pedestrian signal head displays and options for a steady red clearance interval and flashing red buffer interval. - [Image 5] Page 20 is the table of contents locating Chapter 4J (Pedestrian Hybrid Beacons), Sections 4J.01–4J.03. Citations: - Section 4J.03 Standard §2 (p.730) - Section 4J.03 Standard §1 (p.729) - Section 4J.02 Standard §2 (p.728) - Section 4J.03 Guidance §7 (p.730) - Section 4J.03 Option §8 (p.730) - Figure 4J-3 (p.729) - Page 770 - Page 771 - Page 769

**Gold answer:** Flashing circular yellow, then steady circular yellow, then both steady circular red during the walk interval, then alternating flashing circular red during the pedestrian change interval, followed by return to dark.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Each accessible pedestrian push button must have a vibrotactile arrow on the button itself, with high visual contrast, aligned parallel to the direction of travel on the associated crosswalk, so pedestrians with vision disabilities can identify which crossing it actuates and align themselves. A locator tone is also mandatory to help pedestrians locate the vibrotactile arrow and the push button; the tone may be deactivated during the steady DONT WALK display only if passive pedestrian detection reactivates it whenever a pedestrian is within 12 feet. Standards (mandatory): - "Each push button shall clearly indicate by means of a vibrotactile arrow which crosswalk signal is actuated by the push button. Vibrotactile arrows shall be located on the button of the push button assembly, shall have high visual contrast (light on dark or dark on light), and shall be aligned parallel to the direction of travel on the associated crosswalk." - "A locator tone shall be incorporated into the accessible pedestrian signal equipment to help pedestrians with vision disabilities locate the vibrotactile arrow, and the associated push button if a push button is provided." Guidance (recommended): - In temporary traffic control contexts, if a pushbutton is used to provide equivalent TTC information to pedestrians with vision disabilities, it should be equipped with a locator tone to notify pedestrians that a special accommodation is available and to help them locate the pushbutton. Options (permitted): - The locator tone may default to a deactivated mode while the steady UPRAISED HAND (DONT WALK) indication is displayed, provided a passive pedestrian detection system activates the tone whenever a pedestrian is present within a 12-foot radius of the push button (except when the audible walk indication is active); where pedestrian facilities such as sidewalks are present, the passive detection requirement may be reduced to apply only to pedestrians on those facilities within the 12-foot radius. Citations: - Section 4K.04 Standard §1 (p.734) - Section 4K.04 Standard §2 (p.734) - Section 6C.03 Guidance §8 (p.781) - Section 4K.04 Option §5 (p.734) - Section 4K.04 Support §3 (p.734) - Section 4K.01 Support §11 (p.731)

**Gold answer:** The push button must have a high-contrast vibrotactile arrow on the button, aligned parallel to travel on the associated crosswalk. A locator tone must be incorporated; normally it is 0.15 seconds or less and repeats at 1-second intervals when the audible walk indication is not active, subject to stated exceptions.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: They are normally dark and operate only upon pedestrian actuation, stopping after the predetermined period or after a detected pedestrian clears. | When active, they use 75 rapid-flashing sequences per minute. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: RRFBs must remain dark until actuated by a pedestrian, then flash for a predetermined time (or, with passive detection, until the pedestrian clears the crosswalk). All RRFB units serving a crosswalk must start and stop flashing simultaneously, and they must flash at a rapid rate of 75 flashing sequences per minute — a specific exception to normal beacon flash-rate rules. The duration of operation should be based on pedestrian clearance timing procedures. Standards (mandatory): - "The RRFB shall be normally dark, shall initiate operation only upon pedestrian actuation, and shall cease operation at a predetermined time after the pedestrian actuation or, with passive detection, after the pedestrian clears the crosswalk." - "All RRFB units associated with a given crosswalk (including those with an advance crossing sign, if used) shall, when activated, simultaneously commence operation of their rapid flashing indications and shall cease operation simultaneously." - "When activated, the two yellow indications in each RRFB unit shall flash in a rapidly flashing sequence. As a specific exception to the requirements for the flash rate of beacons provided in Paragraph 3 of Section 4S.01, RRFBs shall use a much faster flash rate and shall provide 75 flashing sequences per minute." Guidance (recommended): - The minimum duration of a predetermined period of operation of the RRFBs following each actuation should be based on the procedures for the timing of pedestrian clearance times for pedestrian signals (see Section 4I.06). Options (permitted): - A pedestrian-activated and/or bicyclist-activated RRFB may be used to provide supplemental emphasis to pedestrian, school, and trail warning signs at marked crosswalks across uncontrolled approaches. - At intersections with two crosswalks on an uncontrolled approach, post-mounted RRFBs may be installed to face only one direction of travel at the first crosswalk that traffic encounters; when activated, the RRFBs on both approaches shall simultaneously commence and cease operation. Citations: - Section 4L.03 Standard §1 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Standard §2 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Standard §6 (p.738) - Section 4L.03 Guidance §3 (p.738) - Section 4L.01 Option §1 (p.736) - Section 4L.02 Option §11 (p.738)

**Gold answer:** They are normally dark and operate only upon pedestrian actuation, stopping after the predetermined period or after a detected pedestrian clears. All RRFB units for the crosswalk begin and end together. When active, they use 75 rapid-flashing sequences per minute.

Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 6A.02, Section 8A.13, Section 6A.01, Section 1A.01, p.766, p.768. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Road-user and worker safety and accessibility must be treated as integral, high-priority considerations from planning through design and construction, including motorists, bicycli… Adjudication note: Correct high-priority lifecycle and user coverage; rail-transit coordination is not stated in the direct answer. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.832 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD establishes that road user and worker safety and accessibility should be an integral and high-priority element of every project involving temporary traffic control (TTC), from planning through design and construction. This priority extends to all road users — motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians (including those with disabilities), and workers — and is reflected in the seven fundamental principles of TTC, which call for safety comparable to normal highway conditions and minimal disruption to road user movement. Note that these provisions are Guidance (recommended practice), not mandatory Standards. Guidance (recommended): - Road user and worker safety and accessibility in TTC zones should be an integral and high-priority element of every project from planning through design and construction; maintenance and utility work should be planned and conducted with the safety and accessibility of all motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians (including those with disabilities), and workers considered at all times; if the TTC zone includes a grade crossing, early coordination with the railroad company or light rail transit agency should take place. - General plans or guidelines should be developed to provide safety for motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians, workers, enforcement/emergency officials, and equipment; the basic safety principles governing the design of permanent roadways and roadsides should also govern the design of TTC zones, with the goal of routing road users through such zones using geometrics, roadside features, and TTC devices as nearly as possible comparable to normal highway situations. - A TTC plan, in detail appropriate to the complexity of the work project or incident, should be prepared and understood by all responsible parties before the site is occupied, and any changes should be approved by an official knowledgeable in proper TTC practices; road user movement should be inhibited as little as practical, avoiding frequent and abrupt changes in geometrics that require rapid maneuvers. - Temporary traffic control operations should minimize the inconvenience, delay, and crash potential to affected traffic, and prior notice should be given to affected public or private agencies, emergency services, businesses, railroad companies or transit agencies, and road users before free movement is infringed upon or blocked. Citations: - Section 6A.02 Guidance §1 (p.766) - Section 6A.02 Guidance §2 (p.768) - Section 8A.13 Guidance §7 (p.992) - Section 6A.01 Support §3 (p.765) - Section 8A.13 Support §1 (p.992) - Section 1A.01 Support §1 (p.1)

**Gold answer:** Road-user and worker safety and accessibility must be treated as integral, high-priority considerations from planning through design and construction, including motorists, bicyclists, pedestrians with disabilities, workers, and coordination with rail transit where applicable.

Emergency/incident responders and law enforcement may instead wear ANSI/ISEA 207-2006 public safety vests, and nighttime flaggers should wear Class 3 apparel. Standards (mandatory): - "For daytime and nighttime activity, all workers, including emergency responders, within the right-of-way who are within the TTC zone shall wear high-visibility safety apparel that meets the Performance Class 2 or 3 requirements of the ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 publication entitled 'American National Standard for High-Visibility Safety Apparel and Headwear,' or equivalent revisions, except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section. A person designated by the employer to be responsible for worker safety shall make the selection of the appropriate class of garment." - "When uniformed law enforcement personnel are used to direct traffic, to investigate crashes, or to handle lane closures, obstructed roadways, and disasters, high-visibility safety apparel as described in this Section shall be worn by the law enforcement personnel." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 6 of this Section, firefighters or other emergency responders working within the right-of-way shall wear high-visibility safety apparel as described in this Section." Guidance (recommended): - For flagger wear during nighttime activity, high-visibility safety apparel meeting the Performance Class 3 requirements of ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 (or equivalent revision), and labeled as meeting the ANSI 107-2015 standard performance for Class 3 risk exposure, should be worn. - All on-scene responders and news media personnel should constantly be aware of their visibility to oncoming traffic and wear high-visibility apparel. Options (permitted): - Emergency and incident responders and law enforcement personnel within the TTC zone may wear high-visibility safety apparel meeting the performance requirements of ANSI/ISEA 207-2006 ("American National Standard for High-Visibility Public Safety Vests"), or equivalent revisions, and labeled as ANSI 207-2006, in lieu of ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 apparel. **Gold answer:** Workers, including emergency responders, within the right-of-way and TTC zone must wear ANSI/ISEA 107-2015 Performance Class 2 or 3 apparel, or an equivalent revision, subject to specified responder exceptions. The employer's designated safety person selects the appropriate class.

The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The MUTCD recommends that flaggers, because they are responsible for public safety and have more public contact than any other highway workers, be trained in proper traffic control practices and public contact techniques. A qualified flagger should be able to satisfactorily demonstrate five specific abilities relating to communication, personal safety, device control, traffic control knowledge, and hazard recognition. Guidance (recommended): - Flaggers should be trained in proper traffic control practices and public contact techniques. - Flaggers should be able to satisfactorily demonstrate the following abilities: (A) ability to receive and communicate specific instructions clearly, firmly, and courteously; (B) ability to move and maneuver quickly in order to avoid danger from errant vehicles; (C) ability to control signaling devices (such as paddles and flags) in order to provide clear and positive guidance to drivers approaching a TTC zone in frequently changing situations; (D) ability to understand and apply proper traffic control practices, sometimes in stressful or emergency situations; and (E) ability to recognize dangerous traffic situations and warn workers in sufficient time to avoid injury. - Relatedly, if manual traffic control is needed, it should be provided by qualified flaggers or uniformed law enforcement officers. Citations: - Section 6D.01 Guidance §1 (p.783) - Section 6O.01 Guidance §16 (p.855)

**Gold answer:** A flagger should communicate instructions clearly and courteously; move quickly to avoid danger; control paddles or flags to give clear guidance; understand and apply traffic-control practices under stress; and recognize dangerous situations and warn workers in time.

## T046

**Question:** When two-way traffic must share one lane for a limited distance, what coordination rule and control methods apply?

**Grade:** C=0.80, Comp=0.55, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=No

**Descriptive mean:** 83.8% (Partial credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 76%

**Rationale:** The response received 83.8% as the descriptive mean of the four official item scores. Completeness rationale: The audit found incomplete coverage even though the automated clause comparison did not isolate a single missing sentence. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 6H.17, Section 6E.01, Section 6N.14, Section 6E.02, Section 6E.05, Section 6N.09. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: Movements from both ends must be coordinated, except for a short, low-volume, mutually visible work zone that can self-regulate. Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.947 and Context Precision@6=0.500. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: When traffic in both directions must use a single lane for a limited distance, the movements from each end must be coordinated (with a narrow exception for short, self-regulating situations on low-volume roads). Permitted control methods include flaggers (one or two, depending on visibility) and temporary traffic control signals. Where two-way traffic is carried on one roadway of a normally-divided highway, opposing traffic must be physically separated by specified means—markings and signing alone are not allowed. Standards (mandatory): - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, when traffic in both directions must use a single lane for a limited distance, movements from each end shall be coordinated." - "When two-lane, two-way traffic control must be maintained on one roadway of a normally-divided highway, opposing vehicular traffic shall be separated with either temporary traffic barriers (concrete safety-shape or approved alternate), channelizing devices, Narrow Two-Way Traffic (W6-4) signs on flexible supports (see Section 6H.17), or a temporary raised island throughout the length of the two-way operation. The use of markings and complementary signing, by themselves, shall not be used." Options (permitted): - When a one-lane, two-way TTC zone is short enough for a flagger to see from one end of the zone to the other, traffic may be controlled by a single flagger or by a flagger at each end of the section. - If the work space on a low-volume street or road is short and road users from both directions can see traffic approaching from the opposite direction through and beyond the worksite, movement through the one-lane, two-way constriction may be self-regulating. - Traffic control signals may be used to control vehicular traffic movements in one-lane, two-way TTC zones. Citations: - Section 6E.01 Standard §1 (p.787) - Section 6N.14 Standard §2 (p.850) - Section 6E.02 Option §2 (p.787) - Section 6E.01 Option §4 (p.787) - Section 6E.05 Option §1 (p.788) - Section 6N.09 Support §6 (p.846)

**Gold answer:** Movements from both ends must be coordinated, except for a short, low-volume, mutually visible work zone that can self-regulate. Methods include flaggers, flag transfer, a pilot car, temporary traffic signals, or stop/yield control.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: The assembly or S5-1 sign must be at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced zone begins. | A School (S1-1) sign must be placed in advance of the first school-speed-limit sign in each direction. | The downstream end is normally marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign, with an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) allowed when the two zones end together. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The School Speed Limit assembly (or School Speed Limit When Flashing sign) must be placed at, or as near as practicable to, the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins. In advance of the first such assembly in each direction, a School (S1-1) sign is required (using Table 2C-3 for advance placement). The downstream end of the zone normally must be marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign, although an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign may be substituted in certain circumstances. Standards (mandatory): - "A School Speed Limit assembly (see Figure 7B-1) or a School Speed Limit When Flashing (S5-1) sign (see Figure 7B-1) shall be used to indicate the speed limit where a reduced school speed limit zone has been established based upon an engineering study or where a reduced school speed limit is specified for such areas by statute. The School Speed Limit assembly or School Speed Limit When Flashing sign shall be placed at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced school speed limit zone begins (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "If a reduced school speed limit zone has been established, a School (S1-1) sign shall be installed in advance (see Table 2C-3 for advance placement guidelines) of the first School Speed Limit sign assembly or S5-1 sign that is encountered in each direction as traffic approaches the reduced school speed limit zone (see Figures 7B-2 and 7B-4)." - "Except as provided in Paragraph 4 of this Section, the downstream end of an authorized and posted reduced school speed limit zone shall be identified with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign (see Figures 7B-1, 7B-2, and 7B-4)." - "The static School Speed Limit assembly shall consist of a top plaque (S4-3P) with the legend SCHOOL, a Speed Limit (R2-1) sign, and a bottom plaque (S4-1P, S4-2P, S4-4P, or S4-6P) indicating the specific periods of the day and/or days of the week that the special school speed limit is in effect (see Figure 7B-1)." - "The School Speed Limit assembly shall be either a static sign assembly, a blank-out sign, or a changeable message sign (see Chapter 2L)." Options (permitted): - "The downstream end of a designated school zone may be identified with an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) sign (see Figures 7B-1 and 7B-2)." Visual evidence: - [Image 1] Figure 7B-2 shows the School (S1-1) sign installed in advance of the School Speed Limit assembly (S4-3P/R2-1/S4-1P) at the beginning of the zone, with END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) or END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) signs marking the downstream end in each direction. - [Image 2] Figure 7B-4 similarly shows the S1-1 sign preceding the School Speed Limit assembly and the S5-3 (or S5-2) sign at the downstream end of the reduced school speed limit zone with higher fines plaques. - [Image 3] Figure 7B-1 illustrates the composition of the School Speed Limit assembly (S4-3P SCHOOL plaque, R2-1 Speed Limit sign, and S4-1P/S4-2P/S4-4P/S4-6P bottom plaques) as well as the S5-2 and S5-3 end-of-zone signs. Citations: - Section 7B.05 Standard §1 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §2 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §3 (p.978) - Section 7B.05 Standard §7 (p.980) - Section 7B.05 Standard §6 (p.980) - Section 7B.02 Option §7 (p.976) - Figure 7B-2 (p.975) - Figure 7B-4 (p.979) - Figure 7B-1 (p.973)

**Gold answer:** The assembly or S5-1 sign must be at or as near as practicable to the point where the reduced zone begins. A School (S1-1) sign must be placed in advance of the first school-speed-limit sign in each direction. The downstream end is normally marked with an END SCHOOL SPEED LIMIT (S5-3) sign, with an END SCHOOL ZONE (S5-2) allowed when the two zones end together.

Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: If green pavement is used, it must fill the full limits of the bicycle box. | The box must be at least 10 feet from its upstream edge near the motor-vehicle stop line to its downstream edge near the crosswalk or intersection, must include at least one bicyc… Adjudication note: Correct geometry, symbol, and green-pavement rule; omits the turn-on-red prohibition. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=0.651 and Context Precision@6=0.333. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: A bicycle box must be at least 10 feet deep, measured from its upstream edge (nearest the motor vehicle stop line) to its downstream edge (nearest the crosswalk or intersection), and must contain at least one bicycle symbol marking. Green-colored pavement is optional, but if it is used it must cover the full limits of the bicycle box, and bicycle boxes are one of the only permitted applications of green-colored pavement. The evidence provided does not describe additional operational restrictions (such as turn or signal-phase restrictions) for bicycle boxes. Standards (mandatory): - "If used, the distance from the upstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest to the stop line for motor vehicles to the downstream edge of the bicycle box that is nearest the crosswalk or intersection shall be at least 10 feet. At least one bicycle symbol marking (see Figure 9E-12) shall be used in the bicycle box." - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be used in the full limits of the bicycle box." - "If used, green-colored pavement shall be limited to: A. Bicycle lanes..., B. Extensions of bicycle lanes through intersections..., C. Extensions of bicycle lanes through areas where motor vehicles enter a mandatory turn lane in which motor vehicles must weave across bicyclists in bicycle lanes..., D. Two-stage bicycle turn boxes..., E. Bicycle Boxes..., and F. As a background for bicycle detector symbols...." Guidance (recommended): - If green-colored pavement is used within separated bicycle lanes on an independent alignment, it should be used only at the entrances to those facilities from roadways open to public travel or at conflict, weaving, or crossing locations. Options (permitted): - Green-colored pavement may be installed for the entire length of a bicycle lane or bicycle lane extension, or for only a portion (or portions) of it. (Note: this option addresses bicycle lanes/extensions; for bicycle boxes, the full-limits Standard governs.) Citations: - Section 9E.12 Standard §6 (p.1105) - Section 9E.12 Standard §13 (p.1106) - Section 3H.06 Standard §2 (p.626) - Section 3H.06 Guidance §5 (p.626) - Section 3H.06 Option §7 (p.626) - Section 3H.06 Support §1 (p.625)

**Gold answer:** The box must be at least 10 feet from its upstream edge near the motor-vehicle stop line to its downstream edge near the crosswalk or intersection, must include at least one bicycle symbol, and turns on red must be prohibited from the lane containing the box. If green pavement is used, it must fill the full limits of the bicycle box.

The speed limits displayed shall be in multiples of 5 mph." - "Speed zones (other than statutory speed limits) shall only be established on the basis of an engineering study that has been performed in accordance with traffic engineering practices. Standards (mandatory): - "The abbreviations shown in Table 1D-3 shall not be used in connection with traffic control devices because of their potential to be misinterpreted by road users." - "When the word messages shown in Table 1D-1 need to be abbreviated in connection with traffic control devices, the abbreviations shown in Table 1D-1 shall be used." (i.e., only the approved abbreviations may be substituted, not unacceptable ones such as DLY.) **Gold answer:** It is intended to mean 'Delay' but can be misinterpreted as 'Daily.'

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Per Table 2A-1, the octagon is used exclusively for the Stop (R1-1) sign, the circle exclusively for the Grade Crossing Advance Warning (W10-1) sign, and the pennant (isosceles triangle with longer axis horizontal, pointed right) exclusively for the No Passing Zone (W14-3) sign. Each of these shapes is limited to the sign indicated, and each of those signs must in turn use exclusively the shape shown. Standards (mandatory): - "Particular shapes, as shown in Table 2A-1, shall be used exclusively for specific signs or series of signs, unless otherwise provided in this Manual for a particular sign or class of signs." Table 2A-1 lists: "Octagon* Stop (R1-1)** … Circle* Grade Crossing Advance Warning (W10-1)** Pennant (Isosceles Triangle with longer axis horizontal, pointed right)* No Passing Zone (W14-3)**" - Per the table's footnotes: "* This shape shall be limited exclusively to the sign(s) indicated." and "** This sign shall be exclusively the shape shown." - "Note: Signs with standardized designs shall not be modified to accommodate a different shape except as provided in this Manual." Guidance (recommended): - "Shapes that are exclusive to a particular sign (such as an octagon for STOP, a pennant for NO PASSING ZONE, or a circle for Railroad Advance) should not be obscured by another sign mounted on the back of the same assembly protruding or extending beyond the edge of the sign with the exclusive shape." Methods to consider instead include separate mountings, increasing the size of the exclusive-shape sign, or increasing its mounting height. Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 2A-1 pairing Octagon with Stop (R1-1), Circle with Grade Crossing Advance Warning (W10-1), and Pennant with No Passing Zone (W14-3). [Image 2] shows the full Section 2A.05 Shapes page containing Table 2A-1 and its footnotes. [Image 4] shows the NO PASSING ZONE Sign (W14-3) standard confirming the sign "shall be a pennant-shaped isosceles triangle with its longer axis horizontal and pointing to the right." Citations: - Section 2A.05 Standard §1 (p.42) - Section 2A.05 Guidance §3 (p.42) - Table 2A-1 (p.42)

**Gold answer:** Octagon: STOP; circle: Grade Crossing Advance Warning; pennant: NO PASSING ZONE.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: Table 2A-5 shows that white-on-red signs must maintain a minimum retroreflectivity of W ≥ 35 for the white element and R ≥ 7 for the red element (in cd/lx/m², measured at a 0.2° observation angle and a -4.0° entrance angle); these values apply across all sheeting types in the table. In addition, footnote 4 imposes a minimum sign contrast ratio of ≥ 3:1, calculated as white retroreflectivity ÷ red retroreflectivity. Standards (mandatory): - "Public agencies or officials having jurisdiction shall use an assessment or management method that is designed to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the minimum levels in Table 2A-5." Guidance (recommended): - Except for signs identified in Paragraph 5, one or more of the methods described in "Maintaining Traffic Sign Retroreflectivity" (FHWA-SA-07-020, Revised 2013), FHWA, or a method developed based on an engineering study, should be used to maintain sign retroreflectivity at or above the minimum levels in Table 2A-5; signs identified as being below the minimum levels should be replaced. Options (permitted): - Table 2A-5 lists "White on Red — W ≥ 35; R ≥ 7" applicable across the beaded (Types I, II, III) and prismatic sheeting columns. - Footnote 4 to the table states: "Minimum sign contrast ratio ≥ 3:1 (white retroreflectivity ÷ red retroreflectivity)." - Footnote 1 states the values "are in units of cd/lx/m² measured at an observation angle of 0.2° and an entrance angle of -4.0°." Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows the White on Red row of Table 2A-5 with "W ≥ 35; R ≥ 7" spanning all sheeting-type columns and referencing footnote 4, which specifies the ≥ 3:1 white-to-red contrast ratio; [Image 2] (Page 103) shows the surrounding Section 2A.22 provisions requiring maintenance at or above the Table 2A-5 levels; [Image 3] (Page 104) shows the full Table 2A-5 including the White on Red row and footnotes 1 and 4. Citations: - Table 2A-5 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Option §3 (p.63) - Section 2A.22 Option §4 (p.63) - Section 2A.22 Standard §2 (p.62) - Section 2A.22 Guidance §4 (p.62)

## TB020

**Question:** What is the key difference between automation Levels 3 and 4 in Table 5A-1 regarding a human response to a request to intervene?

**Grade:** C=1.00, Comp=1.00, F=1.00, Cit=1.00, Strict full credit=Yes

**Descriptive mean:** 100.0% (Full credit)

**Evaluation confidence:** 88%

**Rationale:** The response received full credit because the audit accepted the substantive answer, coverage of required elements, grounding, and evidence localization without a deduction. The locked benchmark marks the question as answerable, so the response needed to state and support the required MUTCD answer. Correctness rationale: The response states the locked conclusion, rule, value, or interpretation correctly. Completeness rationale: The audit accepted the response as covering all required answer elements. Faithfulness rationale: The response stays within the locked evidence and does not receive a deduction for unsupported technical claims. Citation rationale: The audit accepted the evidence localization as correct and sufficient. The response identifies: Section 5A.03, Table 5A-1, page 760, p.760, Page 801. Gold elements clearly reflected in the response: At Level 4, the ADS performs the task even if the human driver does not respond appropriately. | At Level 3, the ADS performs the dynamic driving task within its operational design domain with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to in… Retrieval context: The retriever placed at least one locked-relevant text chunk within the top five. The first relevant text chunk appeared at rank 1, which produced MRR=1.000. The six-chunk ordering produced nDCG@6=1.000 and Context Precision@6=0.167. The visual-routing decision matched the locked modality requirement. The saved text, page, or visual package contained enough locked evidence to support an answer. The question required 1 locked visual entity or entities. Exact visual hit=1.00, visual recall=1.00, and visual precision=1.00.

**Model answer:** Direct Answer: The difference lies in whether the automated driving system (ADS) depends on the human driver responding to a request to intervene. At Level 3, the ADS performs all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD "with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene." At Level 4, the ADS performs the driving task "even if a human driver does not respond appropriately to a request to intervene" — that is, the system can handle the situation without human fallback. This distinction is descriptive (Support/table content), not a normative Standard, Guidance, or Option provision. Visual evidence: [Image 1] shows Table 5A-1, where Level 3 is described as "The driving mode specific sustained performance by an ADS of all aspects of the Dynamic Driving Task within a given ODD with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene," while Level 4 reads "…even if a human driver does not respond appropriately to a request to intervene"; both levels fall under the Automated Driving System (ADS) category. [Image 2] shows the full page 760 context, including the Section 5A.03 definitions confirming that "Automated Driving System (ADS)" is the term used specifically for Level 3, 4, or 5 driving automation systems, along with Table 5A-1 itself. Citations: - Table 5A-1 (p.760) - Section 5A.03 Support §1 (p.760) - Page 801

**Gold answer:** At Level 3, the ADS performs the dynamic driving task within its operational design domain with the expectation that the human driver will respond appropriately to a request to intervene. At Level 4, the ADS performs the task even if the human driver does not respond appropriately.
